Multinoulli Extension: A Lossless Continuous Relaxation for Partition-Constrained Subset Selection
Qixin Zhang, Wei Huang, Yan Sun, Yao Shu, Yi Yu, Dacheng Tao

Abstract
Identifying the most representative subset for a close-to-submodular objective while satisfying the predefined partition constraint is a fundamental task with numerous applications in machine learning. However, the existing distorted local-search methods are often hindered by their prohibitive query complexities and the rigid requirement for prior knowledge of difficult-to-obtain structural parameters. To overcome these limitations, we introduce a novel algorithm titled Multinoulli-SCG , which not only is parameter-free, but also can achieve the same approximation guarantees as the distorted local-search methods with significantly fewer function evaluations. More specifically, when the objective function is monotone α \alpha -weakly DR-submodular or ( γ , β ) (\gamma,\beta) -weakly submodular, our Multinoulli-SCG algorithm can attain a value of ( 1 − e − α ) ​ OPT − ϵ (1-e^{-\alpha})\text{OPT}-\epsilon or ( γ 2 ​ ( 1 − e − ( β ⁡ ( 1 − γ ) + γ 2 ) ) β ⁡ ( 1 − γ ) + γ 2 ) ​ OPT − ϵ (\frac{\gamma^{2}(1-e^{-(\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2})})}{\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2}})\text{OPT}-\epsilon with only O ⁡ ( 1 / ϵ 2 ) O(1/\epsilon^{2}) function evaluations, where OPT denotes the optimal value. The cornerstone of our Multinoulli-SCG algorithm is an innovative continuous-relaxation framework named Multinoulli Extension(ME), which can effectively convert the discrete subset selection problem subject to partition constraints into a solvable continuous maximization focused on learning the optimal multinoulli priors across the concerned partition. In sharp contrast with the well-established multi-linear extension for submodular subset selection, a notable advantage of our proposed ME is its intrinsic capacity to provide a lossless rounding scheme for any set function. Furthermore, based on our proposed ME, we also present two novel online algorithms, namely, Multinoulli-OSCG and Multinoulli-OSGA , for the unexplored online subset selection problems over partition constraints. Especially when each incoming set objective function is monotone α \alpha -weakly DR-submodular or ( γ , β ) (\gamma,\beta) -weakly submodular, we verify that both Multinoulli-OSCG and Multinoulli-OSGA can achieve a regret of 𝒪 ⁡ ( T ) \mathcal{O}(\sqrt{T}) against a ( 1 − e − α ) (1-e^{-\alpha}) -approximation or ( γ 2 ​ ( 1 − e − ( β ⁡ ( 1 − γ ) + γ 2 ) ) β ⁡ ( 1 − γ ) + γ 2 ) \big(\frac{\gamma^{2}(1-e^{-(\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2})})}{\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2}}\big) -approximation to the best feasible solution in hindsight, respectively. Here, the symbol T T represents the time horizon. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first result to attain such approximation ratios with 𝒪 ⁡ ( T ) \mathcal{O}(\sqrt{T}) regret to the partition-constrained online α \alpha -weakly DR-submodular or ( γ , β ) (\gamma,\beta) -weakly submodular maximization problems. Finally, extensive simulations and experiments are conducted to demonstrate the effectiveness of our proposed algorithms.



I
Introduction

Subset selection aims to identify a small group of representative items from a vast ground set, which finds numerous real-world applications in the fields of artificial intelligence, machine learning and data mining, including feature selection
qian2015subset
;
das2018approximate
;
dong2025unsupervised
;
dong2025delbo
, data summarization
wei2015submodularity
;
killamsetty2022automata
;
jain2023efficient
;
zhuang2025efficient
, product marketing
kempe2003maximizing
;
borgs2014maximizing
and in-context learning
kumari2024end
;
fan2025combatting
;
li2025otter
. Beyond the aforementioned representational capability, ensuring the diversity and fairness of the chosen subset is of significant importance. For instance, in various marketing scenarios, it is essential to equitably allocate free products across different communities
(
tsang2019group
)
. To this end, partition constraints are often imposed in the process of subset selection, which involves dividing the entire set into non-overlapping sub-classes and then fairly distributing the total budget among them. Motivated by these findings, this paper explores the subset selection problem over partition constraints.

Broadly speaking, the subset selection problem is
NP
-hard
(
natarajan1995sparse
;
feige1998threshold
)
, implying that no polynomial-time algorithms can solve it optimally. In light of this hurdle, many studies have focused on designing efficient approximation algorithms to address the subset selection problem. Especially when the utility function associated with the subset selection problem is
submodular
, a plethora of effective and practical algorithms have been proposed for maximizing this type of functions subject to partition constraints
(
calinescu2011maximizing
;
filmus2014monotone
;
liu2013entropy
)
. Additionally, it has been frequently observed that there are also many scenarios inducing utility functions that are “
close-to-submodular
”, but not strictly submodular. Examples include variable selection for regression
(
elenberg2018restricted
)
, video summarization
(
chen2018weakly
;
li2025language
)
, neural network pruning
(
el2022data
;
he2023structured
;
cheng2024influence
)
and sparse optimal transport
(
montesuma2024recent
;
manupriya2024submodular
)
.

Compared to the extensive literature on submodular maximization, there is a limited amount of research exploring the maximization of “
close-to-submodular
” objectives under partition constraints.
Notably,
(
thiery2022two
)
recently proposed a distorted local-search algorithm to maximize an important class of “
close-to-submodular
” functions named
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular functions and demonstrated that this approach can secure a
\frac{\gamma^{2}(1-e^{-(\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2})})}{\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2}}
-approximation under partition constraints, where
\gamma
and
\beta
represent the lower and upper submodularity ratio respectively. Subsequently,
(
JOGO-Lu
)
extended this local search to another class of “
close-to-submodular
” functions known as
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular functions and also confirmed a tight
(1-e^{-\alpha})
-approximation guarantee under partition constraints, where
\alpha
is the diminishing-return(DR) ratio.

Despite the superior theoretical guarantees of distorted local-search methods, their practical implementation often faces two significant challenges:
i) Reliance on Unknown Parameters:
Distorted local search generally requires prior knowledge of specific structural parameters regarding the objective functions, such as the submodularity ratio and diminishing-return(DR) ratio. However, in practice, accurately estimating these parameters can incur exponential computations.
ii) Prohibitive Query Complexity:
Due to the absence of necessary structural parameters,
(
thiery2022two
;
JOGO-Lu
)
have to adopt a brute-force
\mathcal{O}(1/\epsilon)
-round guesses of these unknown parameters to approximate the distorted local-search methods, which will result in an extremely high
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/\epsilon^{6})
and
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/\epsilon^{3})
number of value queries to the objective function, respectively. In view of all this, a natural question arises:

Q1:
Is it possible to develop a
parameter-free
and
query-efficient
algorithm for the “
close-to-submodular
” subset selection problems under partition constraints while keeping strong approximation guarantees?

In addition, recent years have witnessed a surge in applications related to the
online
subset selection problems over partition constraints. A compelling example is the dynamic deployment of mobile sensors, especially unmanned aerial vehicles(UAVs), to monitor an area of interest. In such scenarios, each UAV needs to constantly determine its trajectory and velocity to effectively track all moving points. The primary challenges of this tracking challenge lie in the unpredictability of the targets’ movements. Notably, under the extended Kalman filter framework, the works
(
hashemi2019submodular
;
hashemi2020randomized
;
rezazadeh2023distributed
)
have pointed out that the aforementioned multi-target tracking problem of UAVs can be formulated as a partition-constrained
online
weakly DR-submodular maximization problem. However, so far, how to extend the state-of-the-art distorted local-search methods
thiery2022two
;
JOGO-Lu
for
offline
weakly DR-submodular maximization into
online
settings still remains an unknown territory. Given this gap, a follow-up question comes to our mind, i.e.,

Q2:
Can we design an effective approximation algorithm for the
online
partition-constrained subset selection problems with “
close-to-submodular
” objectives?

In this paper, we will first provide an affirmative answer to
Q1
by presenting an effective algorithm titled
Multinoulli-SCG
, which not only eliminates the strict requirement for the exact knowledge of both submodularity ratio and DR ratio, but also can attain the same approximation guarantees as the aforementioned distorted local-search methods
(
thiery2022two
;
JOGO-Lu
)
with only
O(1/\epsilon^{2})
function evaluations.
After that, to handle
Q2
, we introduce two novel online algorithms, namely,
Multinoulli-OSCG
and
Multinoulli-OSGA
, for the
online
subset selection problems over partition constraints. Especially when every incoming set objective function is monotone
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular or
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular, we verify that both
Multinoulli-OSCG
and
Multinoulli-OSGA
can achieve a regret of
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{T})
against a
(1-e^{-\alpha})
-approximation or
\big(\frac{\gamma^{2}(1-e^{-(\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2})})}{\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2}}\big)
-approximation to the best solution in hindsight, respectively. Here,
T
is the time horizon. As far as we know, this is the
first
result to achieve such approximation ratios with
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{T})
regret to the partition-constrained
online

\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular or
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular maximization problems. The core of our proposed
Multinoulli-SCG
,
Multinoulli-OSCG
and
Multinoulli-OSGA
algorithms is an innovative continuous-relaxation framework termed as the Multinoulli Extension(
ME
), which aims to learn a prior multinoulli distribution for each community within the concerned partition constraints and subsequently leverages these distributions to make selection. In sharp contrast with the well-established multi-linear extension
(
calinescu2011maximizing
)
, a notable advantage of our proposed
ME
is its inherent capability to provide a lossless rounding scheme for
any
set function. Instead, all known lossless rounding schemes for multi-linear extension
(
calinescu2011maximizing
)
require that the objective set function is
submodular

(
ageev2004pipage
;
chekuri2010submodular
;
chekuri2014submodular
)
.

, ‘

1
),

r\triangleq\sum_{k=1}^{K}B_{k}
,

n=|\mathcal{V}|
and

.

Our Contributions.

1)

This paper introduces a novel continuous relaxation for the partition-constrained subset selection problems, which we term as the Multinoulli Extension(
ME
). Furthermore, we conduct an in-depth exploration of the differentiability, monotonicity and weak submodularity about the
ME
;

2)

When the set objective function is monotone
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular or
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular, we demonstrate that the stationary points of our proposed
ME
can ensure an approximation ratio of
(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{1+\alpha^{2}})
or
(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\beta+\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2}})
to the concerned subset selection problem. To further improve the approximation guarantees of these stationary points, we design two non-oblivious auxiliary functions for the
ME
and prove that the stationary points of these auxiliary functions can provide a better
(1-e^{-\alpha})
-approximation or
\frac{\gamma^{2}(1-e^{-(\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2})})}{\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2}}
-approximation than those of the original
ME
;

3)

To effectively convert the obtained multinoulli distributions into a satisfactory subset within the concern partition constraint, we develop a more practical rounding-without-replacement method for
monotone
set functions;

4)

We propose a novel algorithm named
Multinoulli-SCG
, which effectively integrates our proposed
ME
, the path-integrated differential estimator and the concept of continuous greedy. Moreover, we prove that, when the objective function is monotone
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular or
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular, our
Multinoulli-SCG
can attain a value of
(1-e^{-\alpha})\text{OPT}-\epsilon
or
(\frac{\gamma^{2}(1-e^{-(\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2})})}{\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2}})\text{OPT}-\epsilon
with only
O(1/\epsilon^{2})
function evaluations, where OPT denotes the optimal function value;

5)

By imitating the linear-optimization steps of the
Multinoulli-SCG
algorithm via a sequence of online linear maximization oracles, we present an effective online algorithm named
Multinoulli-OSCG
for the
online
partition-constrained subset selection problems. Furthermore, we establish that, when every incoming set objective function is monotone
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular or
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular, this
Multinoulli-OSCG
can achieve a regret bound of
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{T})
against a
(1-e^{-\alpha})
-approximation or
\big(\frac{\gamma^{2}(1-e^{-(\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2})})}{\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2}}\big)
-approximation to the best solution in hindsight, respectively;

6)

Inspired by the non-oblivious auxiliary functions of our
ME
, we propose a novel variant of the classic online gradient ascent algorithm, named
Multinoulli-OSGA
, for the
online
partition-constrained subset selection problems. Compared to the previous
Multinoulli-OSCG
, this new
Multinoulli-OSGA
not only eliminates the need for maintaining multiple online linear oracles but also can attain the same approximation ratios with
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{T})
regret to the
online
subset selection problems;

7)

We demonstrate the practical efficacy of our proposed algorithms by applying them to video summarization, target tracking and bayesian A-optimal design, etc.

The preliminary results of this paper were presented in part at the 42nd International Conference on Machine Learning in 2025. In this paper, we have significantly expanded the conference version
(
zhang2025multinoulli
)
by
newly
adding the content of the above contributions
2)
,
3)
,
5)
and
6)
.

II
Related Work

Weakly Submodular Maximization:
The
\gamma
-weakly submodular functions were originally introduced by the work
(
das2018approximate
)
, which also demonstrated that the standard greedy algorithm can achieve an approximation ratio of
(1-e^{-\gamma})
for the problem of maximizing such functions subject to a cardinality constraint. Subsequently,
(
chen2018weakly
)
investigate weakly submodular maximization beyond simple cardinality constraint and pointed out that the Residual Random Greedy method of
(
buchbinder2014submodular
)
can achieve an approximation ratio of
\frac{\gamma^{2}}{(1+\gamma)^{2}}
for the problem of maximizing a monotone
\gamma
-weakly submodular functions subject to a matroid constraint. Note that matroid constraint is a natural generalization of the partition constraints considered in this paper. After that,
(
gatmiry2018non
)
examined the approximation performance of standard greedy algorithm on
\gamma
-weakly submodular maximization over matroid constraints, which showed that the standard greedy algorithm can offer an approximation factor of
\frac{0.4\gamma^{2}}{\sqrt{r\gamma}+1}
where
r
is the rank of the matroid. To improve the approximation performance of these greedy algorithms,
(
thiery2022two
)
introduced the notion of upper submodularity ratio
\beta
and also developed a more powerful distorted local search for
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular maximization, which can guarantee a
\frac{\gamma^{2}(1-e^{-(\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2})})}{\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2}}
-approximation for the problem of maximizing a monotone
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular functions subject to a matroid constraint.

Weakly DR-Submodular Maximization:
The work of
(
gatmiry2018non
)
is the first to study the maximization of a
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular subject to general matroid constraints. Specifically,
(
gatmiry2018non
)
proved that the greedy algorithm achieves an
approximation ratio of
\frac{\alpha}{1+\alpha}
for the matroid-constrained
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular maximization. Moreover,
(
ijcai2022p666
)
also considered the impact of curvature
(
sviridenko2017optimal
)
on the standard greedy algorithm for
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular maximization under partition matroid. Recently,
(
JOGO-continuous-loss
)
showed that the continuous greedy combined with the contention resolution scheme
(
chekuri2014submodular
)
can obtain a sub-optimal approaximation ratio of
\alpha(1-1/e)(1-e^{-\alpha})
for the problem of maximizing a monotone
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular functions subject to a matroid constraint. To achieve the tight
(1-e^{-\alpha})
-approximation guarantee,
(
JOGO-Lu
)
recently have proposed a novel distorted local-search method.

Online Submodular Maximization:
The study of online submodular maximization was initiated by
(
streeter2008online
)
, which gave the first algorithm attaining a
(1-e^{-1})
-regret of
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{r\ln(n)T})
for online monotone submodular maximization under a cardinality constraint. Subsequently, this result was generalized to a partition constraint and a general matroid constraint in
(
harvey2020improved
)
with a slightly worse
(1-e^{-1})
-regret of
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{rT\ln(n/r)})
. After that, in order to circumvent the computational bottleneck of repeatedly evaluating and differentiating the multilinear extension,
(
chen2018projection
;
zhang2022boosting
;
pedramfar2023a
;
pedramfar2024from
)
shifted focus to stochastic online continuous DR-submodular maximization problems. Notably, a detailed comparison of our proposed algorithms with existing studies is presented in
Table

I
and
Table

II
.

III
Notations and Preliminaries

In this section we present several important notations and concepts
that we will frequently use throughout this paper.

Notations:
For any positive integer
K
,
[K]
stands for the set
\{1,\dots,K\}
. The symbols
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
and
\|\cdot\|_{2}
denote the inner product and the Euclidean distance, respectively. Similarly, we also use
\|\cdot\|_{0}
and
\|\cdot\|_{1}
to represent the
l_{0}
norm and
l_{1}
norm for vectors, respectively. Moreover,
\Delta_{m}
denotes the standard
m
-dimensional simplex, i.e.,
\Delta_{m}\triangleq\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{m}):\sum_{i=1}^{m}x_{i}\leq 1\ \text{and}\ x_{i}\geq 0,\forall i\in[m]\}
. Especially, the symbol ‘Multi(
\mathbf{p}
)’ represents a multinoulli distribution with
(m+1)
possible states where
\mathbf{p}\in\Delta_{m}
denotes the probability vector. Note that the multinoulli distribution is also known as the categorical distribution in machine learning
(
MLAPP
)
.

Partition of A Set:
Given a finite ground set
\mathcal{V}
, we say
\{\mathcal{V}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{V}_{K}\}
is a partition of set
\mathcal{V}
if and only if i)
\mathcal{V}_{i}\cap\mathcal{V}_{j}=\emptyset
for any
i\neq j\in[K]
; ii)
\mathcal{V}=\bigcup_{k=1}^{K}\mathcal{V}_{k}
.

Subset Selection under Partition Constraints:
Let
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
be a set function that maps any subset of
\mathcal{V}
to a non-negative utility. Given a partition
\{\mathcal{V}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{V}_{K}\}
of
\mathcal{V}
and a collection of budgets
\{B_{1},\dots,B_{K}\}
where
0<B_{k}\leq|\mathcal{V}_{k}|\ \ \forall k\in[K]
, the goal of the subset selection problems subject to partition constraints is aimed at finding a subset
S
from
\mathcal{V}
such that the utility set function
f
is maximized within the constraints
|S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k}|\leq B_{k}
for any
k\in[K]
, i.e.,

\max_{S\subseteq\mathcal{V}}f(S)\ \ \text{s.t.}\ |S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k}|\leq B_{k}\ \ \forall k\in[K].

(1)

Online Partition-Constrained Subset Selection and
\rho
-Regret:
Online subset selection problem is typically formulated as a repeated game between a player and an adversary. Specifically, given a set family
\mathcal{C}
of a ground set
\mathcal{V}
, at each time step
t\in[T]
, the play will first select a subset
S_{t}
from
\mathcal{C}
. Once this choice is committed, the adversary reveals a utility set function
f_{t}:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
to the player. Subsequently, the player receives the reward
f_{t}(S_{t})
and observes the utility set function
f_{t}
. In order to measure the player’s performance, we usually employ the
\rho
-Regret

(
hazan2016introduction
;
streeter2009online
)
metric, that is,

\text{Reg}_{\rho}(T)\triangleq\rho\max_{S\in\mathcal{C}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}f_{t}(S)-\sum_{t=1}^{T}f_{t}(S_{t}),

where
\rho\in(0,1]
denotes the approximation ratio. Note that, in this paper, we only consider the scenarios that the set family
\mathcal{C}
is a partition constraint, i.e.,
\mathcal{C}\triangleq\{S\subseteq\mathcal{V}:|S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k}|\leq B_{k}\ \forall k\in[K]\}
where
\{\mathcal{V}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{V}_{K}\}
is a partition of
\mathcal{V}
.

Monotonicity:
We say that a set function
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is
monotone
if and only if
f(A)\leq f(B)
for any
A\subseteq B\subseteq\mathcal{V}
.

Weak Submodularity:
Given a set function
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
and any two subsets
A,B\subseteq\mathcal{V}
, we denote by
f(B|A)
the marginal contribution of adding the elements of
B
to
A
, i.e.,
f(B|A)\triangleq f(A\cup B)-f(A)
. For simplicity, when
B
is a singleton set
\{v\}
, we also use
f(v|A)
to represent
f(\{v\}|A)
. Therefore, we say that a set function
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is
\gamma
-weakly submodular from below
for some
\gamma\in(0,1]
if and only if, for any two subsets
A\subseteq B\subseteq\mathcal{V}
,

\sum_{v\in B\setminus A}f(v|A)\geq\gamma\Big(f(B)-f(A)\Big),

(2)

where we denote
\gamma
as the
lower submodularity ratio
. Similarly, we also can define the
weak submodularity from above
, that is, a set function
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is
\beta
-weakly submodular from above
for some
\beta\geq 1
if and only if,
\forall A\subseteq B\subseteq\mathcal{V}
,

\sum_{v\in B\setminus A}f(v|B-\{v\})\leq\beta\Big(f(B)-f(A)\Big),

(3)

where
\beta
is called as the
upper submodularity ratio
. When a set function
f
satisfies both Eq.(
2
) and Eq.(
3
), we say it is
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular

(
thiery2022two
)
.

Weak DR-submodularity:
A set function
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular
for some
\alpha\in(0,1]
if and only if
f(v|A)\geq\alpha f(v|B)
for any two subsets
A\subseteq B\subseteq\mathcal{V}
and
v\in\mathcal{V}\setminus B
. In particular,
\alpha
is often called as the diminishing-return(DR) ratio
(
kuhnle2018fast
)
. Note that, from Eq.(
2
) and Eq.(
3
), we can infer that an
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular function automatically satisfies the conditions for being
(\alpha,\frac{1}{\alpha})
-weakly submodular. Moreover, when
\alpha=1
, weakly DR-submodular objectives will reduce to the classical submodular functions
(
nemhauser1978analysis
)
.

IV
Multinoulli Extension

Generally speaking, the discrete nature of subset selection problem (
1
) poses a significant challenge in finding effective solutions. In recent years, compared to discrete optimization, continuous optimization developed an array of efficient and advanced algorithmic tools. Thus, an alternative strategy to address the subset selection problem (
1
) is to bring it into the world of continuous optimization via
relaxation-rounding
frameworks, which typically involve three critical stages: first, converting the problem (
1
) into a solvable continuous optimization; second, applying the gradient-based methods to output a high-quality continuous solution; and third, rounding the previous continuous solution back to the partition constraint of Eq. (
1
) without any loss in terms of the function value. In the subsequent part of this section, we will present a novel relaxation-rounding framework named
M
ultinoulli
E
xtension(
ME
) for problem (
1
).

Prior to this,
(
calinescu2011maximizing
)
proposed a continuous-relaxation technique known as the multi-linear extension for
submodular
subset selection problems. Unfortunately, this extension cannot be directly applied to the general subset selection problem (
1
) because most known lossless rounding schemes for multi-linear extension, such as pipage rounding
(
ageev2004pipage
)
, swap rounding
(
chekuri2010submodular
)
and contention resolution
(
chekuri2014submodular
)
, are heavily dependent on the
submodular
assumption. Up to now, how to losslessly round the multi-linear extension of
non-submodular
set functions, e.g.
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular and
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular functions, still remains an open question
(
thiery2022two
)
. Given the unsolved rounding challenge of multi-linear extension, this paper choose to introduce a new relaxation technique named
M
ultinoulli
E
xtension(
ME
) to address the problem (
1
).

To provide a clearer exposition of our proposed
ME
, we first make some assumptions regarding the problem (
1
): we define
\mathcal{V}_{k}\triangleq\{v_{k}^{1},\dots,v_{k}^{n_{k}}\}
for any
k\in[K]
and set
|\mathcal{V}|=n
, i.e.,
n\triangleq\sum_{k=1}^{K}n_{k}
. More specifically, the core idea of our
ME
is to learn a prior multinoulli distribution ‘Multi(
\mathbf{p}_{k}
)’ for each community
\mathcal{V}_{k}
, where
\mathbf{p}_{k}\triangleq(p_{k}^{1},\dots,p_{k}^{n_{k}})\in\Delta_{n_{k}}
and each
p_{k}^{m}
denotes the probability that element
v_{k}^{m}
is selected within its own community
\mathcal{V}_{k}
for any
m\in[n_{k}]
and
k\in[K]
. Subsequently,
ME
employs each prior distribution ‘Multi(
\mathbf{p}_{k}
)’ to conduct
B_{k}
independent random selections for every community
\mathcal{V}_{k}
, which can ultimately yield a subset that adheres to the partition constraint of problem (
1
). In
Figure

1
, we present a three-community example of
ME
.
It is noteworthy that, with the probability
1-\sum_{m=1}^{n_{k}}p_{k}^{m}
, the multinoulli prior ‘Multi(
\mathbf{p}_{k}
)’ won’t pick any member from
\mathcal{V}_{k}
. In other words, sometimes we might end up with no selection, i.e.,
\emptyset
. Formally, we can define the
ME
as follows:

Fig. 1:
Diagram of Multinoulli Extension.

Definition 1

(Multinoulli Extension)
.

Given a set function
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
, its Multinoulli Extension
F:\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
for problem (
1
) can be defined as:

\displaystyle F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\sim\text{Multi($\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}$)}}\Big(f\big(\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{\hat{k}}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\triangleq\sum_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\}}\Big(f\big(\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{\hat{k}}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\big)\prod_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\prod_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{\hat{k}}}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Big),

where each
e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}
denotes the element chosen at the
\hat{b}
-th random trail of community
\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}},\ \forall\hat{b}\in[B_{\hat{k}}]\forall\hat{k}\in[K]
and the symbol
e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\sim\text{Multi($\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}$)}
indicates that element
e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}

is randomly selected from
\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\}
based on the multinoulli distribution ‘Multi(
\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}
)’, that is,
\text{Pr}(v_{\hat{k}}^{m}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\triangleq p^{m}_{\hat{k}},\ \forall m\in[n_{\hat{k}}],\forall\hat{k}\in[K]
and
\text{Pr}(\emptyset|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\triangleq 1-\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\hat{k}}}p_{\hat{k}}^{m},\ \forall\hat{k}\in[K]
.

Remark 1
.

The introduction of
ME
is aimed at converting the general subset selection problem (
1
) into a continuous maximization task focused on identifying the optimal multinoulli priors across the partition
\{\mathcal{V}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{V}_{K}\}
. Specifically, we hope to address the following continuous optimization:

\max_{p_{k}^{m}\geq 0}F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\ \text{s.t.}\sum_{m=1}^{n_{k}}p_{k}^{m}\leq 1,\forall k\in[K].

(4)

Remark 2
.

In comparison with the multi-linear extension
(
calinescu2011maximizing
)
, a notable advantage of our
ME
is that it does not assign probabilities to any subsets that are out of the partition constraint of problem (
1
). This means that, for
any
set function
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
any
given
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\in\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
, we can, through the definition of
ME
, produce a subset that conforms to the partition constraint of problem (
1
) without any loss in terms of the expected function value
F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})
, where
F
represents the
ME
of
f
. For more details about multi-linear extension, please refer to Appendix
B-A
.

Although the
ME
is naturally endowed with a lossless rounding scheme for
any
set function, there are two crucial questions that must be answered in order to leverage this tool to tackle the subset selection problem (
1
) :
i):

What is the relationship between the function value of our proposed Multinoulli Extension
F
and the original set function
f
?

ii):

How to solve the relaxed problem (
4
)?
For the rest of this section, we will focus on the first question. The exploration of the second question will be presented in Section
V
and
VI
.

IV-A

The Fundamental Properties of Multinoulli Extension

In this subsection, we will concentrate on characterizing several important properties about our proposed
ME
. Specifically, we have the following theorem:

Theorem 1

(Proof provided in Appendix
C-A
)
.

For a set function
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
, its Multinoulli Extension
F:\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
for problem (
1
), as described in the Definition
1
, satisfies the following properties:

1):
The first-order partial derivative of
F
at any point
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\in\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
can be written as follows:

\frac{\partial F}{\partial p_{k}^{m}}\triangleq B_{k}\Bigg(\mathbb{E}_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\sim\emph{Multi(\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})}}\Big(f\Big(v_{k}^{m}\Big|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\Big)\Bigg),

for any
k\in[K]
and
m\in[n_{k}]
;

2):
If
f
is monotone, then
\frac{\partial F}{\partial p_{k}^{m}}\geq 0,\forall k\in[K],m\in[n_{k}]
;

3):
If
f
is
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular, then
F
is
\alpha
-weakly continuous DR-submodular
(
hassani2017gradient
;
zhang2022boosting
)
over the domain
\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
, that is, for any two point
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\in\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
and
(\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\dots,\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{K})\in\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
, if
\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k}\geq\mathbf{p}_{k}
for any
k\in[K]
, we have that

\nabla F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\geq\alpha\nabla F(\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\dots,\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{K});

4):
If
f
is
\gamma
-weakly submodular from below, then
F
is upper-linearizable
(
pedramfar2024from
)
over the domain
\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
, that is, for any two point
\mathbf{x}\triangleq(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\in\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
and
\hat{\mathbf{x}}\triangleq(\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\dots,\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{K})\in\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
, if
\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k}\geq\mathbf{p}_{k}
for any
k\in[K]
, we have that

\gamma\Big(F(\hat{\mathbf{x}})-F(\mathbf{x})\Big)\leq\Big\langle\hat{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{x},\nabla F(\mathbf{x})\Big\rangle.

Remark 3
.

The first point of Theorem
1
provides a characterization about the gradient of our proposed
ME

F
, which indicates that we can estimate
\nabla F
by sampling a sequence of random elements. Specifically, when each
e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}
is independently drawn from the multinoulli distribution ‘Multi(
\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}
)’ for any
\hat{k}\in[K]
and
\hat{b}\in[B_{\hat{k}}]
, we can estimate
\frac{\partial F}{\partial p_{k}^{m}}
as
\widehat{\frac{\partial F}{\partial p_{k}^{m}}}\triangleq B_{k}\left(f\big(v_{k}^{m}\Big|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\big)\right)
.

Remark 4
.

The second point indicates that the mononicity of the set function
f
can be inheritable by its
ME
. Furthermore, the third and fourth points reveal that when the set function
f
exhibits the weak DR-submodularity or weak submodularity, its corresponding
ME
is weakly continuous DR-submodular or upper-linearizable over the domain
\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
.

Remark 5
.

Note that both upper-linearizable and weakly continuous DR-submodular functions defined over the box constraint
[0,1]^{n}
have been extensively studied by
(
hassani2020stochastic
;
hassani2017gradient
;
zhang2022boosting
;
pedramfar2024from
;
wan2023bandit
)
. However, it is crucial to emphasize that these former results cannot be directly applied to our
ME
. This is because all of them require the domain of objective functions to be closed under the coordinate-wise maximum operation
\vee
, i.e.,
\mathbf{x}\vee\mathbf{y}\triangleq\max(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
. Unfortunately, the domain
\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
of our proposed
ME
does not meet this requirement. For further details, please refer to Appendix
B-B
.

Next, we uncover the relationship between the function value of our proposed
ME

F
and that of the original
f
. To be more precise, we have the following theorem:

Theorem 2

(Proof provided in Appendix
C-B
)
.

When the set function
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is monotone and
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular, for any subset
S
within the partition constraint of problem (
1
) and any point
\mathbf{x}\triangleq(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\in\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
, the following inequality holds:

\alpha\Big(f(S)-F(\mathbf{x})\Big)\leq\left\langle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{B_{k}}\mathbf{1}_{(S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k})},\nabla F(\mathbf{x})\right\rangle,

where the symbol
\mathbf{1}_{S}
is the indicator function over the set
S
, meaning that, for any element
v_{k}^{m}\in S
, the vector
\mathbf{1}_{S}
sets the corresponding coordinate of its probability
p_{k}^{m}
to be
1
; otherwise,
0
. Similarly, when the set function
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is monotone and
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular, for any subset
S
within the partition constraint of problem (
1
) and any point
\mathbf{x}\triangleq(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\in\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
, we can infer that

\gamma^{2}f\big(S\big)-(\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2})F\big(\mathbf{x}\big)\leq\left\langle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\mathbf{1}_{(S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k})}}{B_{k}},\nabla F(\mathbf{x})\right\rangle.

Remark 6
.

Theorem
2
implies that when
f
is monotone and
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular, for any
S
within the partition constraint of problem (
1
) and any point
\mathbf{x}\in\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
,
the discrepancy between
f(S)
and
F(\mathbf{x})
can be bounded by the inner product
\left\langle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{B_{k}}\mathbf{1}_{(S\cap\mathcal{V}_{i})},\nabla F(\mathbf{x})\right\rangle
. Similarly, if
f
is monotone and
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular, this inner product
\left\langle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{B_{k}}\mathbf{1}_{(S\cap\mathcal{V}_{i})},\nabla F(\mathbf{x})\right\rangle
also can bound the gap between
\gamma^{2}f(S)
and
(\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2})F(\mathbf{x})
.

IV-B

Stationary Point and Non-oblivious Auxiliary Functions

Broadly speaking, with the aid of
ME

F
, a feasible strategy for solving the subset selection problem (
1
) is to initially apply the off-the-shelf gradient-based methods, such as gradient ascent and Frank Wolfe, to the relaxed problem (
4
), and subsequently, to finalize our selection by rounding the resulting continuous solution. Furthermore, as extensively documented in the literature
(
hassani2017gradient
;
hassani2020stochastic
)
, under mild conditions, a wide range of gradient-based algorithms will converge to the stationary points of their target objectives. Motivated by these findings, we next investigate the stationary points of our proposed
ME
.

Before going into the details, we firstly revisit the definition of stationary points for maximization problem, that is,

Definition 2
.

Given a differentiable objective function
G:\mathcal{K}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and a domain
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{K}
, a point
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{M}
is called as a stationary point for the function
G
over the domain
\mathcal{M}
if and only if
\max_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{M}}\langle\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x},\nabla G(\mathbf{x})\rangle\leq 0
.

Next, we will detail the specific performance of the stationary points of our proposed Multinoulli Extension relative to the maximum value of problem (
1
). Specifically, we have that

Theorem 3

(Proof provided in Appendix
D-A
)
.

If the set function
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is monotone and
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular, then for any stationary point
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})
of its Multinoulli Extension
F
over the domain
\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
, the following inequality holds:

F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\geq\Big(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{1+\alpha^{2}}\Big)f(S^{*}),

where
S^{*}
is the optimal solution of problem (
1
). Similarly, when the set function
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is monotone and
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular, for any stationary point
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})
over the domain
\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
, we also can show that

F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\geq\Big(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\beta+\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2}}\Big)f(S^{*}).

Remark 7
.

It is worth noting that the approximation ratios established in Theorem
3
is tight for stationary points. In Appendix
D-B
, we will present a simple instance of a submodular function, i.e.,
c=\alpha=\gamma=\beta=1
, whose
ME
can attain a
(1/2)
-approximation guarantee at a stationary point.

Fig. 2:
(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{1+\alpha^{2}})
v.s.
(1-e^{-\alpha})
.

As revealed by Theorem
3
, when the set function
f
is monotone
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular or
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular, the direct deployment of gradient-based algorithms targeting at stationary points on the relaxed problem (
4
) only can secure an approximation ratio of
(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{1+\alpha^{2}})
or
(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\beta+\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2}})
relative to the optimum of the subset selection problem (
1
). In comparison,
(
harshaw2019submodular
)
demonstrated that the optimal approximation guarantee for maximizing monotone
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular functions is
(1-e^{-\alpha})
, which substantially outperforms the aforementioned
(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{1+\alpha^{2}})
-approximation provided by stationary points of
ME

F
, as demonstrated in Figure
2
. This significant disparity naturally raises the question:
Can we devise approaches to close this non-negligible gap?

Previously, the works
(
filmus2014monotone
;
zhang2022boosting
;
wan2023bandit
)
effectively employed the well-established
Non-Oblivious Search
technique to achieve improved solution quality via the design of specialized auxiliary functions. Motivated by these advances, we also hope to construct a surrogate function capable of strengthening the approximation bounds for stationary points of our proposed
ME
. Following the methodology developed in
(
filmus2014monotone
;
zhang2022boosting
;
wan2023bandit
)
, we consider a type of auxiliary functions
F^{a}(\mathbf{x})
re-weights gradients of our
ME
at scaled locations
z*\mathbf{x}
through the following formulation:

\nabla F^{a}(\mathbf{x})\triangleq\int_{0}^{1}w(z)\nabla F(z*\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}z,

(5)

where
w(z)
denotes a positive weighting function defined on
[0,1]
. After carefully
selecting
w(z)
, we can establish that:

Theorem 4

(Proof provided in Appendix
D-C
)
.

When
f
is monotone
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular and
w(z)\triangleq e^{\alpha(z-1)}
, for any stationary point
(\mathbf{p}^{a}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}^{a}_{K})
of the auxiliary function
F^{a}
over the domain
\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
, the following inequality holds:

F(\mathbf{p}^{a}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}^{a}_{K})\geq\big(1-e^{-\alpha}\big)f(S^{*}),

where
S^{*}
is the optimal solution of problem (
1
). Similarly, if
f
is monotone
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular and
w(z)\triangleq e^{\phi(\gamma,\beta)(z-1)}
where
\phi(\gamma,\beta)\triangleq\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2}
, for any stationary point
(\mathbf{p}^{a}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}^{a}_{K})
of the auxiliary function
F^{a}
over the domain
\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
, then we can show that

F(\mathbf{p}^{a}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}^{a}_{K})\geq\big(\frac{\gamma^{2}(1-e^{-\phi(\gamma,\beta)})}{\phi(\gamma,\beta)}\big)f(S^{*}).

Theorem

4
indicates that, when the set function
f
is monotone
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular or
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular, the direct application of gradient-based
methods targeting stationary points to the auxiliary function
F^{a}
can achieve a tight approximation ratio of
(1-e^{-\alpha})
or
\big(\frac{\gamma^{2}(1-e^{-\phi(\gamma,\beta)})}{\phi(\gamma,\beta)}\big)
to the subset selection problem (
1
), respectively.

IV-C

Rounding without Replacement for Monotone Set Functions

Algorithm 1

Rounding without Replacement

Input:
Priors
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})
, budgets
\{B_{1},\dots,B_{K}\}
and partition
\{\mathcal{V}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{V}_{K}\}
of set
\mathcal{V}

1:

for

k=1,\dots,K

do

2:

if

\|\mathbf{p}_{k}\|_{1}>0

then

\triangleright
Normalization (Lines 2-6)

3:

Normalize
\mathbf{p}_{k}\triangleq\frac{\mathbf{p}_{k}}{\|\mathbf{p}_{k}\|_{1}}
;

4:

else

5:

Set
\mathbf{p}_{k}\triangleq(\frac{1}{n_{k}},\dots,\frac{1}{n_{k}})\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{k}}
;

6:

end

if

\triangleright
Sampling without Replacement (Lines 7-8)

7:

Compute
B^{k}_{min}\triangleq\min(\|\mathbf{p}_{k}\|_{0},B_{k})
;

8:

Utilize multinoulli prior
\mathbf{p}_{k}
to
sample

B^{k}_{min}
different elements
\{e_{k}^{1},\dots,e_{k}^{B^{k}_{min}}\}
from
\mathcal{V}_{k}\triangleq\{v_{k}^{1},\dots,v_{k}^{n_{k}}\}

without replacement
;

9:

if

B^{k}_{min}<B_{k}

then

\triangleright

Complement Sampling(Lines 9-11)

10:

Generate
B_{k}-B^{k}_{min}
elements
\{e_{k}^{B^{k}_{min}+1},\dots,e_{k}^{B_{k}}\}

randomly
from
\mathcal{V}_{k}\setminus\{e_{k}^{1},\dots,e_{k}^{B^{k}_{min}}\}
;

11:

end

if

12:

end

for

13:

Return

S\triangleq\cup_{k=1}^{K}\cup_{b=1}^{B_{k}}\{e_{k}^{b}\}
;

In the previous two subsections, we have thoroughly investigated the relationships between our proposed
ME

F
and its original set function
f
, which provides a solid theoretical foundation for finding exceptional multinoulli priors
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})
for the relaxed problem (
4
). However, a critical question remains:
how can we losslessly and effectively transfer these resulting multinoulli priors
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})
into a subset of our target partition constraint?
A straightforward approach, as outlined in
Definition

1
, is to independently utilize each multinoulli prior
\mathbf{p}_{k}
a total of
B_{k}
times to generate candidates
\{e_{k}^{1},\dots,e_{k}^{B_{k}}\}
from
\mathcal{V}_{k}\cup\{\emptyset\}
. Yet, there is a chance that two different candidates may choose the same element from
\mathcal{V}_{k}
or some candidate may end up being empty, thereby causing the final subset size to be strictly less than
B_{k}
, i.e.,
|\cup_{b=1}^{B_{k}}\{e_{k}^{b}\}|<B_{k}
. Particularly when the original set function
f
is strictly
monotone
, such issues will significantly impair the practical effectiveness of our final selected subset. To overcome this drawback, we propose a simple and effective rounding-without-replacement method for
monotone
set objective functions, as detailed in
Algorithm

1
.
This method not only ensures that the final selected subset contains exactly
B_{k}
distinct elements in each
\mathcal{V}_{k},\forall k\in[K]
, but can guarantee that the function value of the resulting subset is greater than that of the corresponding multinoulli priors, that is to say,

Theorem 5

(Proof in Appendix
E-A
)
.

When the set function
f
is monotone, for any multinoulli priors
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\in\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
, we can show that the subset
S
yielded by
Algorithm

1
satisfies the following properties:
1):

|S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k}|\equiv B_{k}
for any
k\in[K]
;
2):

\mathbb{E}(f(S))\geq F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})
where
F
is the multinoulli extension of
f
for problem (
1
).

V
Continuous Greedy Algorithms for Subset Selection over Partition Constraints

In this section, we delve into the development of effective
offline
and
online
approximation algorithms for the partition-constrained subset selection problems based on our previously introduced
M
ultinoulli
E
xtension(
ME
). Specifically, in subsection
V-A
, we propose a
parameter-free
and
query-efficient
algorithm titled
Multinoulli-SCG
for our concerned subset selection problem (
1
). After that, in subsection
V-B
, we utilize the idea of meta-action
(
streeter2009online
;
chen2018online
;
harvey2020improved
)
to extend this
Multinoulli-SCG
algorithm into more complicated
online
settings.

V-A

Stochastic Variant of Continuous Greedy Algorithm

This subsection aims to present an effective method named
Multinoulli-SCG
to maximize our introduced
ME
, which is primarily inspired by the
continuous greedy
(CG) algorithm for the multi-linear extension
(
calinescu2011maximizing
)
.

The CG algorithm typically comprises two critical steps: First, it begins with
\mathbf{x}(1)\triangleq\textbf{0}
and then, at each iteration
t\in[T]
, the algorithm identifies the optimal ascent direction
\mathbf{v}(t)\triangleq\mathop{\arg\max}_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{M}}\left\langle\mathbf{v},\nabla G\big(\mathbf{x}(t)\big)\right\rangle
to update the current variable
\mathbf{x}(t)
according to the rule
\mathbf{x}(t+1)\triangleq\mathbf{x}(t)+\frac{1}{T}\mathbf{v}(t)
where
G
is the multi-linear extension of a set function
f
and
\mathcal{M}
is a convex domain. The motivation behind the CG algorithm is that when
f
is
monotone submodular
, the inequality
\left\langle\mathbf{y},\nabla G\big(\mathbf{x}\big)\right\rangle\geq G(\mathbf{y})-G(\mathbf{x})
holds for any feasible
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
. Note that, in Theorem
2
, we establish a similar relationship between our proposed
ME

F
and its original set function
f
, that is , the inner product
\left\langle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{B_{k}}\mathbf{1}_{S\cap\mathcal{V}_{i}},\nabla F(\mathbf{x})\right\rangle
can bound a specific form of weighted discrepancy between
f(S)
and
F(\mathbf{x})
, when
f
is monotone
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular or
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular. Motivated by these findings, we naturally consider a two-step variant of CG algorithm to maximize our proposed
ME
: Initially, we set
\mathbf{x}(1)\triangleq\mathbf{0}
, and then, at each iteration
t\in[T]
, we find the optimal subset
S(t)\triangleq\mathop{\arg\max}_{S\subseteq\mathcal{V},|S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k}|\leq B_{k},\forall k\in[K]}\left\langle\nabla F(\mathbf{x}(t)),\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\mathbf{1}_{(S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k})}}{B_{k}}\right\rangle
to update the current variable
\mathbf{x}(t)
according to the rule
\mathbf{x}(t+1)\triangleq\mathbf{x}(t)+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{B_{k}}\mathbf{1}_{(S(t)\cap\mathcal{V}_{k})}
.

However, the implementation of the aforementioned two-step algorithm requires accurately computing the gradients of our proposed
ME

F
, which is typically computationally intensive. To circumvent this obstacle, we adopt the stochastic path-integrated differential estimator
(
hassani2020stochastic
;
fang2018spider
)
, namely, for a sequence of iterations
\{\mathbf{x}(t)\}_{t=1}^{T+1}
, we estimate each gradient
\nabla F\big(\mathbf{x}(t)\big)(t\geq 2)
by the following path-integral form:
\widetilde{\nabla}F\big(\mathbf{x}(t)\big)\triangleq\nabla F\big(\mathbf{x}(1)\big)+\sum_{s=2}^{t}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{s}
,
where each
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{s}
is an unbiased estimator for the difference
\textbf{Diff}_{s}\triangleq\nabla F(\mathbf{x}(s))-\nabla F(\mathbf{x}(s-1))
. Note that, when
F
is twice differentiable, for any
2\leq s\leq T+1
, the difference
\textbf{Diff}_{s}
can be rewritten as:

\textbf{Diff}_{s}\triangleq\int_{0}^{1}\nabla^{2}F(\mathbf{x}^{a}(s))\mathrm{d}a\Big(\mathbf{x}(s)-\mathbf{x}(s-1)\Big),

where
\mathbf{x}^{a}(s)\triangleq a\mathbf{x}(s)+(1-a)\mathbf{x}(s-1),\forall a\in[0,1]
and
\nabla^{2}F
is the Hessian of
F
. Hence, if we uniformly sample the parameter
a
from
[0,1]
, the difference
\textbf{Diff}_{s}
can be unbiasedly estimated by
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{s}\triangleq\widetilde{\nabla}^{2}F(\mathbf{x}^{a}(s))\Big(\mathbf{x}(s)-\mathbf{x}(s-1)\Big)
where
\widetilde{\nabla}^{2}F
is an unbiased estimator of
\nabla^{2}F
. Following this idea, we proceed to demonstrate how to estimate the second-order derivative of our proposed
ME

F
, that is to say,

Theorem 6

(Proof provided in
Section

C-C
)
.

For a set function
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
, the second-order derivative of its Multinoulli Extension
F
at any point
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\in\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
can be written as follows:

1):
If
k_{1}\neq k_{2}\in[K]
, for any
m_{1}\in[n_{k_{1}}]
and
m_{2}\in[n_{k_{2}}]
,

\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial p_{k_{1}}^{m_{1}}\partial p_{k_{2}}^{m_{2}}}\triangleq B_{k_{1}}B_{k_{2}}\mathbb{E}\Big(f\Big(v_{k_{1}}^{m_{1}}\Big|S\cup\{v_{k_{2}}^{m_{2}}\}\Big)-f\Big(v_{k_{1}}^{m_{1}}\Big|S\Big)\Big),

where
S\triangleq\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\left\{(k_{1},1),(k_{2},1)\right\},\hat{k}\in[K],\hat{b}\in[B_{\hat{k}}]}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}
and each
e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}
is drawn from the multinoulli distribution Multi(
\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}
);

2):
As for
k_{1}=k_{2}=k\in[K]
, if
B_{k}\equiv 1
, for any
m_{1},m_{2}\in[n_{k}]
, we have
\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial p_{k}^{m_{1}}\partial p_{k}^{m_{2}}}\triangleq 0
; Moreover, when
B_{k}\geq 2
,

\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial p_{k}^{m_{1}}\partial p_{k}^{m_{2}}}\triangleq(B_{k}^{2}-B_{k})\mathbb{E}\Big(f\Big(v_{k}^{m_{1}}\Big|S\cup\{v_{k}^{m_{2}}\}\Big)-f\Big(v_{k}^{m_{1}}\Big|S\Big)\Big),

where
S\triangleq\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\left\{(k,1),(k,2)\right\}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}
and each
e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}
is independently drawn from the multinoulli distribution Multi(
\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}
).

Remark 8
.

Theorem
6
provides a detailed characterization of the second-order derivative of our proposed
ME

F
, which implies that we can estimate the Hessian of
F
by sampling a sequence of random elements. Specifically, when each
e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}
is independently drawn from the multinoulli distribution ‘Multi(
\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}
)’ for any
\hat{k}\in[K]
and
\hat{b}\in[B_{\hat{k}}]
, we can estimate
\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial p_{k_{1}}^{m_{1}}\partial p_{k_{2}}^{m_{2}}}(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})
as:
when
k_{1}\neq k_{2}\in[K]
,

\widehat{\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial p_{k_{1}}^{m_{1}}\partial p_{k_{2}}^{m_{2}}}}\triangleq B_{k_{1}}B_{k_{2}}\Big(f\Big(v_{k_{1}}^{m_{1}}\Big|S\cup\{v_{k_{2}}^{m_{2}}\}\Big)-f\Big(v_{k_{1}}^{m_{1}}\Big|S\Big)\Big),

where
S=\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\left\{(k_{1},1),(k_{2},1)\right\},\hat{k}\in[K],\hat{b}\in[B_{\hat{k}}]}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}
; As for
k_{1}=k_{2}=k\in[K]
and
B_{k}\geq 2
,

\widehat{\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial p_{k}^{m_{1}}\partial p_{k}^{m_{2}}}}\triangleq(B_{k}^{2}-B_{k})\Big(f\Big(v_{k}^{m_{1}}\Big|S\cup\{v_{k}^{m_{2}}\}\Big)-f\Big(v_{k}^{m_{1}}\Big|S\Big)\Big),

where
S=\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\left\{(k,1),(k,2)\right\},\hat{k}\in[K],\hat{b}\in[B_{\hat{k}}]}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}
.

Incorporating this second-order approximation and the idea of differential estimator into the previously mentioned two-step variant of CG algorithm, we can develop a stochastic algorithm for maximizing our
ME
, as detailed in Algorithm
2
.

Algorithm 2

Stochastic Continuous Greedy Algorithm for Multinoulli Extension(
Multinoulli-SCG
)

Input:
Batch size
L
, number of iterations
T
, set function
f
and partition
\{\mathcal{V}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{V}_{K}\}
of set
\mathcal{V}

1:

Initialize:

\mathbf{x}(1)=\left(\mathbf{p}_{1}(1),\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}(1)\right)=\textbf{0}_{n}
;

2:

for

t=1,\dots,T

do

3:

if

t=1

then

\triangleright
Differential Estimator(Lines 3-12)

4:

Compute
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}(1)\triangleq\nabla F(\textbf{0}_{n})
based on
Theorem

1
;

5:

else

6:

Sample
\{a(1),\dots,a(L)\}
uniformly from
[0,1]
;

7:

Set
\mathbf{x}_{l}(t)\triangleq a(l)\mathbf{x}(t)+(1-a(l))\mathbf{x}(t-1),\forall l\in[L]
;

8:

Compute the Hessian estimator
\widehat{\nabla}^{2}F\big(\mathbf{x}_{l}(t)\big)
based on Remark
8
for any
l\in[L]
;

9:

Compute
\widehat{\nabla}^{2}_{t}\triangleq\frac{1}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\widehat{\nabla}^{2}F\big(\mathbf{x}_{l}(t)\big)
;

10:

Compute
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}\triangleq\widehat{\nabla}^{2}_{t}\big(\mathbf{x}(t)-\mathbf{x}(t-1)\big)
;

11:

Aggregate the estimator
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}(t)=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}(t-1)+\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}
;

12:

end

if

\triangleright
Stochastic Continuous Greedy(Lines 13-14)

13:

S(t)\triangleq\mathop{\arg\max}_{|S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k}|\leq B_{k}}\left\langle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}(t),\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{B_{k}}\mathbf{1}_{S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k}}\right\rangle
;

14:

Update
\mathbf{x}(t+1)\triangleq\mathbf{x}(t)+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{B_{k}}\mathbf{1}_{S(t)\cap\mathcal{V}_{k}}
;

15:

end

for

16:

Return

S
by rounding
\mathbf{x}(T+1)
without replacement;

Furthermore, based on the results of Theorem
1
and Theorem
2
, we can show that

Theorem 7

(Proof provided in Appendix
F-A
)
.

When the set function
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is monotone and
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular, if we set the batch size
L=\mathcal{O}(T)
, the subset
S
output by Algorithm
2
satisfies:

\mathbb{E}\big(f(S)\big)\geq\Big(1-e^{-\alpha}\Big)f(S^{*})-\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{r\sqrt{n}}{T}\Big),

where
S^{*}
is the optimal solution of problem (
1
),
r
is the rank of partition constraint, i.e.,
r=\sum_{k=1}^{K}B_{k}
and
n=|\mathcal{V}|
. Similarly, if the set function
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is monotone
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular and
L=\mathcal{O}(T)
, the subset
S
output by Algorithm
2
satisfies:

\mathbb{E}\big(f(S)\big)\geq\Big(\frac{\gamma^{2}(1-e^{-(\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2})})}{\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2}}\Big)f(S^{*})-\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{r\sqrt{n}}{T}\Big).

Remark 9
.

Theorem
7
implies that, when the set function
f
is
\alpha
-weakly monotone DR-submodular, if we set
T=\mathcal{O}(1/\epsilon)
, the subset yielded by our proposed Algorithm
2
can secure a value of
(1-e^{-\alpha})\text{OPT}-\epsilon
, where OPT is the maximum value of problem (
1
). Moreover, when
f
is
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly monotone submodular, Algorithm
2
also can achieve
\big(\frac{\gamma^{2}(1-e^{-(\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2})})}{\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2}}\big)\text{OPT}-\epsilon
after
\mathcal{O}(1/\epsilon)
iterations.

Remark 10
.

It is important to note that the optimization objective
\left\langle\nabla F(\mathbf{x}(t)),\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\mathbf{1}_{(S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k})}}{B_{k}}\right\rangle
in line 13 is a modular set function with respect to
S
. As a result, we can employ a greedy method to efficiently find the optimal subset
S(t)\triangleq\mathop{\arg\max}_{S\subseteq\mathcal{V},|S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k}|\leq B_{k},\forall k\in[K]}\left\langle\nabla F(\mathbf{x}(t)),\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\mathbf{1}_{(S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k})}}{B_{k}}\right\rangle
.

Remark 11
.

From Line 13, we know that each
S(t)
has at most
r
non-zero entries, where
r=\sum_{k=1}^{K}B_{k}
. Moreover, from lines 10 and 14, we also can know
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}\triangleq\widehat{\nabla}^{2}_{t}\big(\mathbf{x}(t)-\mathbf{x}(t-1)\big)\triangleq\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{TB_{k}}\left(\widehat{\nabla}^{2}_{t}\mathbf{1}_{\left(S(t-1)\cap\mathcal{V}_{k}\right)}\right)
. Hence, the computation of
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}
only utilizes
\mathcal{O}(nr)
entries in the Hessian estimation
\widehat{\nabla}_{t}^{2}
. This implies that it is sufficient to estimate up to
\mathcal{O}(nr)
second-order partial derivatives for each point
\mathbf{x}_{l}(t)
at Line 8.

From
Remark

9
and
Remark

11
, we know that when
f
is monotone
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular or
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular, if we set the batch size
L=\mathcal{O}(T)
and
T=\mathcal{O}(\frac{r\sqrt{n}}{\epsilon})
, our Algorithm
2
can attain a
(1-e^{-\alpha})\text{OPT}-\epsilon
or
\big(\frac{\gamma^{2}(1-e^{-(\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2})})}{\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2}}\big)\text{OPT}-\epsilon
approximation of the offline subset selection problem (
1
) with
\mathcal{O}(\frac{r^{3}n^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}})
function evaluations to the objective function
f
, respectively. Compared to the local-search methods
(
thiery2022two
;
JOGO-Lu
)
, Algorithm
2
not only eliminates the need for prior knowledge of parameters
\alpha
,
\gamma
, and
\beta
, but also can reduce the dependence on the error parameter
\epsilon
from the previous
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/\epsilon^{6})
or
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/\epsilon^{3})
to
\mathcal{O}(1/\epsilon^{2})
.

V-B

Online Variant of Continuous Greedy Algorithm

In this subsection, we aim to extend our previous
Algorithm

2
into online settings. Compared to offline scenarios, the primary challenge in online settings is that, at each moment
t\in[T]
, the utility set function
f_{t}
remains completely unknown until we commit to a subset
S_{t}
. If we assume every incoming utility objective function
f_{t}
were known in advance at each timespot
t\in[T]
, then we could have utilized
Algorithm

2
, e.g., running it for
Q
-round iterations, to maximize the multinoulli extension
F_{t}
of
f_{t}
. Specifically, at each
q
-th iteration where
q\in[Q]
, we would have found an optimal subset
S_{t}(q)\triangleq\mathop{\arg\max}_{S\subseteq\mathcal{V},|S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k}|\leq B_{k},\forall k\in[K]}\left\langle\nabla F_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t}(q)),\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\mathbf{1}_{(S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k})}}{B_{k}}\right\rangle
to update the current variable
\mathbf{x}_{t}(q)
according to the rule
\mathbf{x}_{t}(q+1)\triangleq\mathbf{x}_{t}(q)+\frac{1}{Q}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{B_{k}}\mathbf{1}_{(S_{t}(q)\cap\mathcal{V}_{k})}
. However due to the unknown
f_{t}
, it is impossible to find such subset
S_{t}(q)
.

Previously, when extending the
continuous greedy
(CG) algorithm
(
calinescu2011maximizing
)
for multi-linear extension into online settings,
(
streeter2009online
;
chen2018online
;
harvey2020improved
)
also encountered a similar issue, namely, we must make decisions at each moment without prior knowledge of future
submodular
set functions. To address this,
(
streeter2009online
;
chen2018online
;
harvey2020improved
)
proposed a novel technique named meta-action. The core idea of meta-action is to mimic the linear-optimization step of CG algorithm, i.e.,
\max_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{M}}\left\langle\mathbf{v},\nabla G_{t}\big(\mathbf{x}_{t}(q)\big)\right\rangle
, by a sequence of online linear maximization algorithms
\{\mathcal{E}^{(q)}:1\leq q\leq Q\}
, where
G_{t}
is the multi-linear extension of the corresponding set function
f_{t}
and
\mathcal{M}
is a convex domain. More specifically, at each time
t\in[T]
,
(
streeter2009online
;
chen2018online
;
harvey2020improved
)
choose to utilize the output
\mathbf{v}_{t}(q)\in\mathcal{M}
of
q
-th linear oracle
\mathcal{E}^{(q)}
as a surrogate of the optimal vector of problem
\max_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{M}}\left\langle\mathbf{v},\nabla G_{t}\big(\mathbf{x}_{t}(q)\big)\right\rangle
and then perform the iteration
\mathbf{x}_{t}(q+1)\triangleq\mathbf{x}_{t}(q)+\frac{1}{Q}\mathbf{v}_{t}(q)
for any
q\in[Q]
. After committing the final
\mathbf{x}_{t}(Q+1)
,
(
streeter2009online
;
chen2018online
;
harvey2020improved
)
learns the
f_{t}
and feeds back the product
\left\langle\mathbf{v}_{t}(q),\nabla G\big(\mathbf{x}_{t}(q)\big)\right\rangle
as the payoff to each linear oracle
\mathcal{E}^{(q)},\forall q\in[Q]
.

Motivated by the idea of meta-action, we also employ a sequence of online linear maximization algorithms
\{\mathcal{E}^{(q)}:1\leq q\leq Q\}
to extend the
offline

Algorithm

2
into online settings. It is worth noting that when the set
S
is within the partition constraint of problem (
1
), the update direction
\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{B_{k}}\mathbf{1}_{(S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k})}
in Line 14 of
Algorithm

2
lies within the domain
\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
. As a result, we designate each oracle
\mathcal{E}^{(q)},\forall q\in[Q]
as an online linear maximization algorithm over
\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
and then, at each time
t\in[T]
, we use the output
\mathbf{v}_{t}(q)
of the
q
-th oracle
\mathcal{E}^{q}
to replace the update direction
\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{B_{k}}\mathbf{1}_{(S_{t}(q)\cap\mathcal{V}_{k})}
, as detailed in
Algorithm

3
.

Algorithm 3

Online Stochastic Continuous Greedy Algorithm for Multinoulli Extension(
Multinoulli-OSCG
)

Input:
Number of oracles
Q
, batch size
L
, time horizon
T
and
\{\mathcal{V}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{V}_{K}\}
of set
\mathcal{V}

1:

Initialize:

Q

\{\mathcal{E}^{(1)},\dots,\mathcal{E}^{(Q)}\}
for maximizing linear functions over the domain
\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
;

2:

Initialize:

\mathbf{x}_{t}(1)\triangleq\textbf{0}_{n}
for any
t\in[T]
;

3:

for

t=1,\dots,T

do

4:

for

q=1,\dots,Q

do

\triangleright
Continuous Greedy(Lines 4-7)

5:

Obtain the output
\mathbf{v}_{t}(q)
from the oracle
\mathcal{E}^{(q)}
;

6:

Update

;

7:

end

for
\triangleright
Rounding and Commitment(Lines 8-9)

8:

Obtain
S_{t}
by rounding
\mathbf{x}_{t}(Q+1)
without replacement;

9:

Commit to
S_{t}
, receive reward
f_{t}(S_{t})
and observe
f_{t}
;

10:

for

q=1,\dots,Q

do

11:

if

q=1

then

\triangleright
Differential Estimator(Lines 11-20)

12:

Compute
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{t}(1)\triangleq\nabla F_{t}(\textbf{0}_{n})
based on
Theorem

1
;

13:

else

14:

Sample
\{a_{t}(1),\dots,a_{t}(L)\}
uniformly from
[0,1]
;

15:

Compute
\mathbf{x}_{t}^{(l)}(q)\triangleq a_{t}(l)\mathbf{x}_{t}(q)+(1-a_{t}(l))\mathbf{x}_{t}(q-1)
for any
l\in\{1,2,\dots,L\}
;

16:

Compute Hessian estimator
\widehat{\nabla}^{2}F_{t}\big(\mathbf{x}_{t}^{(l)}(q)\big)
based on Remark
8
for any
l\in\{1,2,\dots,L\}
;

17:

Compute
\widehat{\nabla}^{2}_{t}(q)\triangleq\frac{1}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\widehat{\nabla}^{2}F_{t}\big(\mathbf{x}_{t}^{(l)}(q)\big)
;

18:

Compute
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}(q)\triangleq\widehat{\nabla}^{2}_{t}(q)\big(\mathbf{x}_{t}(q)-\mathbf{x}_{t}(q-1)\big)
;

19:

Aggregate the estimator
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{t}(q)\triangleq\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{t}(q-1)+\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}(q)
;

20:

end

if
\triangleright
Update Online Linear Oracle(Line 21)

21:

Feed back
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{t}(q)
to the linear oracle
\mathcal{E}^{(q)}
;

22:

end

for

23:

end

for

Similar to
Algorithm

2
, in Lines 11-20 of
Algorithm

3
, we utilize a path-integrated differential estimator
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{t}(q)
to approximate each gradient
\nabla F_{t}\big(\mathbf{x}_{t}(q)\big)
for any
q\in[Q]
and
t\in[T]
, where
F_{t}
is the
ME
of the
t
-th objective function
f_{t}
. After that, like the meta-action, the Line 21 of
Algorithm

3
feeds back all obtained gradient estimations
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{t}(q),\forall q\in[Q]
to their corresponding linear oracles
\mathcal{E}^{(q)},\forall q\in[Q]
.

Next, we provide the theoretical analysis for
Algorithm

3
. Before that, we introduce some standard assumptions about the linear maximization oracles
\{\mathcal{E}^{(q)}:q\in[Q]\}
, namely,

Assumption 1
.

Each linear maximization oracle
\mathcal{E}^{(q)},\forall q\in[Q]
can achieve a
1
-Regret
of
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{nr}\sqrt{T})
where
r
is the rank of partition constraint of problem (
1
), that is,
r\triangleq\sum_{k=1}^{K}B_{k}
,
n\triangleq|\mathcal{V}|
and
T
is the time horizon.

Remark 12
.

It is worth noting that there exist several off-the-shelf algorithms that can achieve a regret bound of
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{nr}\sqrt{T})
for online linear maximization problem, for instance, Online Gradient Ascent
(
zinkevich2003online
)
and Regularized-Follow-The-Leader
(
hazan2016introduction
)
.

With this
1
, we then can get the following convergence results for our
Algorithm

3
, that is,

Theorem 8

(Proof provided in Appendix
F-B
)
.

If
1
holds, when each objective set function
f_{t}
is monotone
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular or
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular, the
\rho
-Regret of our proposed
Algorithm

3
satisfies that

\mathbb{E}\left(\text{Reg}_{\rho}\left(T\right)\right)\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\sqrt{nr}\sqrt{T}+r\sqrt{n}\big(\frac{T}{L}+\frac{T}{Q}\big)\right),

where
\rho=(1-e^{-\alpha})
or
\rho=\frac{\gamma^{2}(1-e^{-(\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2})})}{\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2}}
, respectively. Note that
L
represents the batch size for differential estimators and
Q
denotes the number of linear maximization oracles.

Remark 13
.

Theorem
8
suggests that, when every incoming set function
f_{t}
is monotone
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular or
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular, if we set the batch size
L=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{rT})
and employ
Q=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{rT})
linear maximization oracles,
Algorithm

3
can achieve a regret of
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{nr}\sqrt{T})
against a
(1-e^{-\alpha})
-approximation or
\big(\frac{\gamma^{2}(1-e^{-(\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2})})}{\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2}}\big)
-approximation to the best feasible solution in hindsight, respectively.

Remark 14
.

Note that recent works
(
harvey2020improved
;
fang2022online
)
proposed a more efficient
O
nline
D
ual
A
veraging(ODA) algorithm for the online linear maximization problem over the domain
\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
and demonstrated that it can achieve a
1
-regret of
\mathcal{O}\big(\sqrt{rT\ln(n/r)}\big)
. Thus, in
Algorithm

3
, if all linear oracles
\mathcal{E}^{(q)},q\in[Q]
adopt this ODA algorithm, then when each set objective function
f_{t}
is monotone
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular or
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular, the
\rho
-Regret of
Algorithm

3
satisfies:

\mathbb{E}\left(\text{Reg}_{\rho}\left(T\right)\right)\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\sqrt{r\ln(n/r)}\sqrt{T}+r\sqrt{n}\big(\frac{T}{L}+\frac{T}{Q}\big)\right).

In particular, when
L=Q=\mathcal{O}\big(\sqrt{\frac{rn}{\ln(n/r)}}\sqrt{T}\big)
,
Algorithm

3
can achieve an improved
\rho
-regret bound of
\mathcal{O}\big(\sqrt{rT\ln(n/r)}\big)
.

Remark 15
.

To the best of our knowledge, this is the
first
result that not only attains a
(1-e^{-\alpha})
-regret of
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{T})
for online
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular maximization problems over a partition constraint, but also can achieve a
\big(\frac{\gamma^{2}(1-e^{-(\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2})})}{\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2}}\big)
-approximation to the online partition-constrained
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular maximization problems.

VI
Stochastic Gradient Ascent for Subset Selection over Partition Constraints

Although the idea of meta-action enables us to extend the
offline

Algorithm

2
to more complex online settings, it is important to note that, in
Algorithm

3
, we need to maintain
Q
distinct online linear maximization algorithms
\{\mathcal{E}^{(1)},\dots,\mathcal{E}^{(Q)}\}
, which can result in substantial memory overhead. More critically, at the end of each round
t\in[T]
,
Algorithm

3
requires computing
Q
differential estimators
\{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{t}(1),\dots,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{t}(Q)\}
to update the introduced oracles
\{\mathcal{E}^{(1)},\dots,\mathcal{E}^{(Q)}\}
, which will incur a total of
LQ
Hessian approximations for each
ME

F_{t}
, thereby leading to a significant number of function evaluations for every set objective
f_{t}
. To address these issues, an alternative algorithmic scheme is to employ the Online Gradient Ascent(OGA)
(
zhang2022boosting
;
zinkevich2003online
)
, whose framework is not only simple to implement but more query-efficient. Thus, in the subsequent part of this paper, we focus on designing an effective and efficient OGA algorithm for the partition-constrained subset selection problem.

Note that, when every incoming set function
f_{t}
is a fixed set objective
f
, the classic OGA algorithm will reduce to
offline
gradient ascent algorithm. Furthermore, it is well-known that
offline
gradient ascent with a sufficiently small step size will converge to a stationary point of its target objective function. As a result, from the results of
Theorem

3
, we can know that directly applying OGA to the
ME

F_{t}
of every incoming objective set function
f_{t}
only can ensure a
(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{1+\alpha^{2}})
-approximation or
(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\beta+\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2}})
-approximation to the best feasible solution in hindsight, which is weaker than the previously established approximation ratios of
(1-e^{-\alpha})
or
\big(\frac{\gamma^{2}(1-e^{-(\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2})})}{\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2}}\big)
provided by
Theorem

8
. Fortunately, as demonstrated in
Theorem

4
, the stationary points of auxiliary function
F^{a}
can attain the same approximation guarantees as those proven in
Theorem

8
. Here,
\nabla F^{a}(\mathbf{x})\triangleq\int_{0}^{1}w(z)\nabla F(z*\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}z
where
F
is a
ME
of some set function
f
and
w(z)
is the positive weight function over
[0,1]
. Motivated by these findings, we then choose to apply the OGA algorithm into the auxiliary functions
F_{t}^{a}
of the
ME

F_{t}
of every set objective function
f_{t}
, as shown in
Algorithm

4
.

Algorithm 4

Online Stochastic Gradient Ascent Algorithm for Multinoulli Extension(
Multinoulli-OSGA
)

Input:
Step size
\eta
, Partition
\{\mathcal{V}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{V}_{K}\}
, Ratios
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
, Budgets
\{B_{1},\dots,B_{K}\}

1:

Initialize:

\mathbf{x}_{1}\triangleq\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{n_{k}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{V}_{k}}
and
\text{Auxiliary}\triangleq\textbf{True}
;

2:

for

t\in[T]

do

\triangleright
Rounding and Commitment(Lines 2-3)

3:

Obtain
S_{t}
by rounding
\mathbf{x}_{t}
without replacement;

4:

Commit to
S_{t}
, receive reward
f_{t}(S_{t})
and observe
f_{t}
;

5:

if

not

Auxiliary

then

\triangleright
Gradient Estimation(Lines 6-7)

6:

Compute the gradient estimator
\widehat{\nabla}F_{t}\big(\mathbf{x}_{t}\big)
based on the
Remark

3
;

7:

Set
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{t}\triangleq\widehat{\nabla}F_{t}\big(\mathbf{x}_{t}\big)
;

8:

else

\triangleright
Auxiliary Gradient Estimation(Lines 9-11)

9:

Generate a random number
z_{t}
from r.v.
\mathcal{Z}
where
\text{Pr}(\mathcal{Z}\leq z)\triangleq\frac{1}{\int_{0}^{1}w(a)\mathrm{d}a}\int_{0}^{z}w(a)\mathrm{d}a,\forall z\in[0,1]
;

10:

Compute the gradient estimator
\widehat{\nabla}F_{t}\big(z_{t}*\mathbf{x}_{t}\big)
based on the
Remark

3
;

11:

Set
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{t}\triangleq(\int_{a=0}^{1}w(a)\mathrm{d}a)\widehat{\nabla}F_{t}\big(z_{t}*\mathbf{x}_{t}\big)
;

12:

end

if
\triangleright
Stochastic Gradient Ascent(Lines 13-14)

13:

Compute
\mathbf{y}_{t+1}=\mathbf{x}_{t}+\eta\boldsymbol{\mathrm{g}}_{t}
;

14:

Update
\mathbf{x}_{t+1}\triangleq\mathop{\arg\min}_{\mathbf{x}\in\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}}\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}_{t+1}\|_{2}

15:

end

for

16:

Return

S_{l}
and
l\triangleq\mathop{\arg\max}_{t\in[T]}f(S_{t})
;
\triangleright
Offline Setting

Notably, in Lines 9-11 of
Algorithm

4
, we employ a two-step sampling method to estimate each gradient
\nabla F^{a}_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t})
. Specifically, we initially samples a random number
z_{t}
from the random variable
\mathcal{Z}
with distribution
\text{Pr}(\mathcal{Z}\leq z)\triangleq\frac{\int_{0}^{z}w(a)\mathrm{d}a}{\int_{0}^{1}w(a)\mathrm{d}a},\forall z\in[0,1]
and then approximates each
\nabla F^{a}_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t})
by
(\int_{a=0}^{1}w(a)\mathrm{d}a)\widehat{\nabla}F_{t}\big(z_{t}*\mathbf{x}_{t}\big)
where
\widehat{\nabla}F_{t}\big(z_{t}*\mathbf{x}_{t}\big)
is an unbiased estimator of
\nabla F_{t}\big(z_{t}*\mathbf{x}_{t}\big)
. Additionally,
Algorithm

4
provides a flexible option to directly use OGA algorithm over the
ME

F_{t}
instead of its auxiliary function
F_{t}^{a}
by the “Auxiliary” variable. Next, we provide the theoretical analysis for
Algorithm

4
.

Theorem 9

(Proof provided in Appendix
G-A
)
.

When every objective set function
f_{t}
is monotone
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular or
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular, if we utilize the standard gradient estimation(Lines 6-7), namely
\text{Auxiliary}\triangleq\textbf{False}
, the
\rho
-Regret of our proposed
Algorithm

4
satisfies that

\mathbb{E}\left(\text{Reg}_{\rho}\left(T\right)\right)\leq\mathcal{O}\big(r/\eta+nT\eta\big),

where
\rho=\frac{\alpha^{2}}{1+\alpha^{2}}
or
\rho=\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\beta+\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2}}
, respectively. Note that
r
is the rank of partition constraint, i.e.,
r\triangleq\sum_{k=1}^{K}B_{k}
,
n=|\mathcal{V}|
and
\eta
is the step size. Similarly, if we use the auxiliary gradient estimation(Lines 9-11), i.e.,
\text{Auxiliary}\triangleq\textbf{True}
, and set the weight function
w(z)
according to
Theorem

4
, the
\rho
-Regret of our proposed
Algorithm

4
satisfies that

\mathbb{E}\left(\text{Reg}_{\rho}\left(T\right)\right)\leq\mathcal{O}\big(r/\eta+nT\eta\big),

where
\rho=(1-e^{-\alpha})
or
\rho=\frac{\gamma^{2}(1-e^{-(\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2})})}{\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2}}
, respectively.

Remark 16
.

Theorem
9
suggests that, when every incoming set function
f_{t}
is monotone
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular or
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular, if we adopt the auxiliary gradient estimation and set the step size
\eta\triangleq\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\frac{r}{nT}})
,
Algorithm

4
can achieve a regret of
\mathcal{O}\big(\sqrt{nr}T^{1/2}\big)
against a
(1-e^{-\alpha})
-approximation or
\big(\frac{\gamma^{2}(1-e^{-(\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2})})}{\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2}}\big)
-approximation to the best feasible solution in hindsight, respectively.

Remark 17
.

A byproduct of
Theorem

9
is that, in
offline
settings, if we return the final subset as the Line 16 of
Algorithm

4
and set the step size
\eta=\mathcal{O}(\frac{\epsilon}{n})
, then when the set objective function
f
is monotone
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular or
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular, the subset
S_{l}
yielded by our proposed
Algorithm

4
also can secure a value of
\rho\cdot\text{OPT}-\epsilon
after
\mathcal{O}(\frac{nr}{\epsilon^{2}})
iterations, where OPT is the maximum value of problem (
1
) and
\rho
denotes the obtained approximation ratio. For further details, please refer to Appendix
G-B
.
