VII-A

Offline Subset Selection

In this subsection, we empirically compare the performance of our proposed
Multinoulli-SCG
,
Multinoulli-SGA
and
Multinoulli-ASGA
against the standard greedy method
(
gatmiry2018non
)
and the residual random greedy method
(
chen2018weakly
)
as well as the distorted local search
(
thiery2022two
)
across three distinct applications: video summarization, special maximum coverage and bayesian A-optimal design. Note that
Multinoulli-SGA
and
Multinoulli-ASGA
respectively represent the
offline
classical and auxiliary stochastic gradient ascent applied to our proposed
ME
(See
Algorithm

4
).
Due to the space
limits, the detailed experimental setups are provided in
Appendix

A
.

VII-A
1
Video Summarization

The objective of video summarization is aimed at picking a few representative frames from a given video such that these frames can capture as much content as possible. To achieve this, a common strategy is to formulate the frame selection problem as the maximization of a
D
eterminantal
P
oint
P
rocess(DPP) objective function
(
gong2014diverse
;
mirzasoleiman2018streaming
;
chen2018weakly
)
. DPP has recently emerged as a powerful tool that favors subsets of a ground set of items with higher diversity
(
kulesza2012determinantal
)
. More specifically, for an
n
-frame video, we represent each frame by a
p
-dimensional vector. Then, we compute the Gramian matrix
X
of the
n
resulting vectors by setting each
X_{ij}
as the Gaussian kernel between the
i
-th and
j
-th vectors. With this matrix
X
, the DPP objective function can be defined as
f(S)=\text{det}(I+X_{S})
where
S\subseteq[n]
,
X_{S}
is the principal submatrix of
X
indexed by
S
and
I
is a
|S|
-dimensional identity matrix. Note that
(
bian2017guarantees
)
has proven that this set function
f
is monotone and weakly submodular from below. Moreover,
(
ijcai2022p666
)
also verified the weak DR-submodularity of
f
.

For our experiments, we use six videos from the VSUMM dataset
(
de2011vsumm
)
and two videos about ‘Animation’ and ‘Cooking’ from websites like YouTube. Additionally, we utilize the method described in
(
gong2014diverse
)
to prune each video, namely, for long videos(
\geq 5
min), we uniformly sampled one frame per second, and for short videos, we sampled one frame every half second. Subsequently, we choose to create a summary of each video by extracting one representative frame from every
25
frames, that is, we consider the following partition constraint:

\big|S\cap[25(i-1)+1,25i]\big|\leq 1\ \ \ \ \ \forall\ 1\leq i\leq\lceil n/25\rceil.

Table

III
illustrates the performance of our proposed
Multinoulli-SCG
and
Multinoulli-SGA
algorithms against three benchmark methods, namely, ‘Standard Greedy’, ‘Residual-Greedy’ and ‘Distorted-LS-G’. It is quite easy to observe that our
Multinoulli-SCG
and
Multinoulli-SGA
algorithms produce summaries with higher diversity than the other three baselines. Specifically,
Multinoulli-SCG
achieves Top-2 performance in all 8 videos, while
Multinoulli-ASGA
and
Multinoulli-SGA
attain Top-1 performance on at least 6 out of the 8 videos. Furthermore, the number of function evaluations required by our
Multinoulli-SCG
,
Multinoulli-SGA
and
Multinoulli-ASGA
is 2, 4 and 4 orders of magnitude lower than that of the state-of-the-art ‘Distorted-LS-G’, respectively. This result aligns well with our previous theoretical findings in
Section

V
and
Section

VI
.

VII-A
2
Special Maximum Coverage

In video summarization tasks, it is easily observed that our
Multinoulli-SGA
algorithm exhibits exceptional empirical performance. However, according to both
Theorem

3
and
Theorem

9
, we know that our
Multinoulli-SGA
only can ensure a sub-optimal approximation guarantee in the worst case. To verify the theoretical correctness of our
Multinoulli-SGA
algorithm, we consider a special maximum coverage problem as discussed in
Section

D-B
.

Let the universe set
U
consist of
n-1
elements
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1}\}
and
n-k
elements
\{y_{1},\dots,y_{n-k}\}
, all of weight
1
, and
n-1
elements
\{\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{n-1}\}
of arbitrarily
small weight
\epsilon>0
. Then, we define two different set
A_{i}
and
B_{i}
for any
i\in[n]
, that is to say ,

\displaystyle A_{i}\triangleq\{\epsilon_{i}\}\text{\ for $1\leq i\leq n-1$,}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ A_{n}\triangleq\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1}\},

\displaystyle B_{i}\triangleq\{x_{i}\}\text{\ for $1\leq i\leq n-1$,}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ B_{n}\triangleq\{y_{1},\dots,y_{n-k}\}.

After that, we define a coverage set function
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
over these
2n
distinct set
\{A_{1},\dots,A_{n},B_{1},\dots,B_{n}\}
where
\mathcal{V}=[2n]
. Specifically, we have that, for any subset
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
,

f(\mathcal{F})=\sum_{v\in\bigcup_{j\in\mathcal{F}}S_{j}}w(v),

(6)

where
w(v)
is the weight of element
v
,
S_{j}=A_{j}
when
1\leq j\leq n
and
S_{j}=B_{j-n}
as for
n+1\leq j\leq 2n
.

Moreover, we consider a partition constraint that contains at most one of
\{A_{i},B_{i}\}
for any
i\in[n]
. If we set
\mathcal{V}_{i}=\{i,i+n\}
(
\mathcal{V}=\bigcup_{i\in[n]}\mathcal{V}_{i}=[2n]
), we naturally obtain the following coverage maximization problem:

\max_{\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{V}}f(\mathcal{F})\ \ \text{s.t.}\ |\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{V}_{i}|\leq 1\ \ \forall i\in[n].

(7)

From the result of
(
filmus2012power
)
, we know that the problem (
27
) is a submodular maximization problem subject to a partition matroid constraint, namely,
\alpha=\beta=\gamma=1
. A key feature of this coverage maximization problem is that we found that the
point
\mathbf{1}_{[n]}
is a local
(1/2)
-approximation stationary point of the
ME
of the cover function
f
(See
Theorem

12
in
Section

D-B
).

Motivated by this finding, we empirically compare the performance of our proposed
Multinoulli-SCG
,
Multinoulli-SGA
and
Multinoulli-ASGA
against the standard greedy method and the residual random greedy method as well as the distorted local search across distinct coverage maximization (
27
) with different
n
and
k
, where we uniformly set
\epsilon_{i}=0.01,1\leq i\leq n-1
.

\log_{10}
of the total number of value queries to the set objective function, with a smaller value being more favorable. In each column of ‘obj’,

\blacksquare
indicates ranking the 1st and

, we found that both our proposed ‘

\{A_{1},\dots,A_{n}\}
. Moreover, the ‘Residual-Greedy’ method oscillates between the optimal subset

. Similar to the video summarization in

VII-A
3
Bayesian A-Optimal Design

We begin by outlining the formulation of Bayesian A-optimal design
hashemi2019submodular
;
borsos2024data
. Consider an unknown parameter vector
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
to be estimated from noisy linear observations via least squares regression. Our objective is to choose a set
S
of linear measurements which have low cost and also maximally reduce the variance of
our OLS estimate. Formally, let
X=[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
be a matrix including
n
candidate measurement vectors. Given a set of
measurement vectors
S\subseteq[n]
, we run the experiments and obtain the noisy linear
observations
y_{S}=X_{S}^{T}\theta+\epsilon_{S}
, where
\epsilon_{S}\sim N(\textbf{0},\sigma^{2}I_{|S|})
. Then, the OLS estimate of
\theta
becomes
\hat{\theta}=(X_{S}X_{S}^{T})^{-1}X_{S}^{T}y_{S}
. Specially, under a Gaussian prior
\theta\sim N(\textbf{0},\Sigma)
, the total estimation variance equals
r(S)=tr(\Sigma+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}X_{S}X_{S}^{T})^{-1}
where
tr(\cdot)
is the trace of a matrix. By defining the variance reduction
g(S)=r(\emptyset)-r(S)=tr(\Sigma)-tr(\Sigma+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}X_{S}X_{S}^{T})^{-1}
, we can reformulate the bayesian A-optimal design as a (
\gamma
,
\beta
)-weakly submodular maximization problem
thiery2022two
with
\beta=1/\gamma
.

In our experiment, we evaluate our methods on four datasets from
(
chang2011libsvm
)
, that is, Housing, Eunite2001, Ionosphere and Sonar. Like
(
harshaw2019submodular
)
, we preprocessed the data by normalizing the features to have a zero mean and a standard deviation of
1
. After that, we set
\sigma=1/\sqrt{d}
and randomly generated a normal prior with covariance
\Sigma=ADA^{T}
where
d
is the dimension of data point,
A\sim N(\textbf{0},I)
,
D
is a diagonal matrix with
D_{i,i}=(i/d)^{2}
. As for the partition constraint, we randomly cut the all data points into
10
different groups and then select at most one element from each group. Then, we present the results of bayesian A-optimal design in
Table

IV
.

From last four columns of
Table

IV
, we found that the performance of our proposed
Multinoulli-SCG
and
Multinoulli-SGA
algorithms surpasses that of three benchmark methods, namely, ‘Standard Greedy’, ‘Residual-Greedy’ and ‘Distorted-LS-G’. Furthermore, like the previous experiments about video summarization and maximum coverage, the number of value queries to the set function required by our
Multinoulli-SCG
,
Multinoulli-SGA
and
Multinoulli-ASGA
is 2, 4 and 4 orders of magnitude lower than that of the state-of-the-art ‘Distorted-LS-G’ algorithm
(
thiery2022two
;
JOGO-Lu
)
, respectively.

VII-B

Online Subset Selection

Fig. 3:
Target Tracking via UAVs

In this subsection, we test the effectiveness of our proposed
Multinoulli-OSCG
and
Multinoulli-OSGA
via simulated multi-target tracking tasks, as depicted in
Figure

3
. In this scenario, at each moment, a centralized control unit needs to coordinate the actions of multiple mobile sensors, particularly unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs), to effectively track all moving targets of interest. Due to the space
limits, the detailed experimental setups are provided in
Section

A-B
.

VII-B
1
Tracking with Facility-Location Objectives

Following experimental protocols from
(
zhang2025nearoptimal
;
xu2023online
)
, we deploy 20 UAVs in a planar environment to track 30 mobile targets over a 25-second horizon, partitioned into
T\triangleq 1250
discrete iterations. Per iteration, each UAV determines both a movement direction from the action set {up, down, left, right, diagonal} and a speed from the discrete levels {5, 10, 15} units/s. Target behaviors are classified into three categories: the stochastic ‘Random’ type, the structured ‘Polyline’ type, and the strategically evasive ‘Adversarial’ type. ‘Random’ targets draw their heading angle
\theta
uniformly from
[0,2\pi]
and sample velocities independently from
[5,10]
units/s at each step. ‘Polyline’ targets maintain deterministic trajectories except at waypoint iterations
\{0,\lfloor\frac{T}{k}\rfloor,2\lfloor\frac{T}{k}\rfloor,\dots,(k-1)\lfloor\frac{T}{k}\rfloor\}
(with
k\in\{1,2,4\}
drawn randomly), where they temporarily exhibit Random behavior. ‘Adversarial’ targets behave identically to ‘Random’ targets when all agents remain beyond 20 units distance; upon detecting any agent within this 20-unit threshold, they initiate a one-second evasion at 15 units/s toward the direction maximizing the average separation from all agents.

(a)

‘R’:‘A’:‘P’=4:5:1

(b)

‘R’:‘A’:‘P’=6:3:1

(c)

‘R’:‘A’:‘P’=8:1:1

(d)

Extended Kalman Filter

(e)

0.1
-Network Search

Fig. 4:
A comparison of the running average utility on different multi-target tracking scenarios.

Generally speaking, every motion of any UAV can be characterized by three key parameters, namely, its movement angle
\theta
, speed
s
and the unique identifier
i
. With all these three parameters, then the action set
\mathcal{V}_{i}
available to each UAV
i\in[20]
can be mathematically represented as:

\mathcal{V}_{i}\triangleq\left\{(\theta,s,i):s\in\{5,10,15\}\text{units/s},\theta\in\{\frac{\pi}{4},\frac{\pi}{2},\dots,2\pi\}\right\},

where each tuple
(\theta,s,i)
encodes a specific action of UAV
i
, that is, it will move at a speed of
s
in the direction of
\theta
. Furthermore, at each time step
t\in[T]
, we denote the location of each target
j\in[30]
as
o_{j}(t)
. Similarly, we also utilize the symbol
o_{(\theta,s,i)}(t)
to represent the new position of UAV
i
after moving from its previous location at time
(t-1)
with a movement angle
\theta
and speed
s
.

To enhance the tracking quality, a common strategy is to minimize the distances between agents and targets. Inspired by this idea, many studies
(
xu2023online
;
zhang2025nearoptimal
)
naturally consider the following facility-location objective for UAVs at every time
t\in[T]
, i.e.,

f_{t}(S)\triangleq\sum_{j=1}^{30}\max_{(\theta,s,i)\in S}\frac{1}{\|o_{(\theta,s,i)}(t)-o_{j}(t)\|_{2}},

where
\|o_{(\theta,s,i)}(t)-o_{j}(t)\|_{2}
represents the Euclidean distance between the location
o_{j}(t)
of target
j
and the new position
o_{(\theta,s,i)}(t)
after UAV
i
executing the action
(\theta,s,i)
and
S
is a subset of the ground action set
\mathcal{V}\triangleq\cup_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{V}_{i}
. It is important to note that the larger the value of
\frac{1}{\|o_{(\theta,s,i)}(t)-o_{j}(t)\|_{2}}
becomes, the closer the action
(\theta,s,i)
drives UAV
i
to the target
j
.

Considering this facility-location utility set function
f_{t}
and the truth that each UAV only can execute one decision from
\mathcal{V}_{i}
at every time
t\in[T]
, then we can easily transform the aforementioned multi-target tracking problem as a partition-constrained subset selection problem, namely,

\max f_{t}(S),\ \ \text{ s.t.}\ S\subseteq\mathcal{V}\ \text{and}\ |S\cap\mathcal{V}_{i}|\leq 1,\forall i\in[20].

(8)

Furthermore, numerous studies
(
xu2023online
;
zhang2025nearoptimal
)
have verified that this facility-location objective function
f_{t}
is
monotone submodular
.

In simulations, we initialize the starting positions of all UAVs and targets randomly within 20-unit radius circle centered at the origin. Furthermore, we consider different proportions of ‘
R
andom’, ‘
P
olyline’, and ‘
A
dversarial’ targets. Specifically, we set the proportions of targets as ‘
R
andom’:‘
A
dversarial’:‘
P
olyline’=
4
:
5
:
1
in
Figure

4(a)
and
6
:
3
:
1
in
Figure

4(b)
as well as
8
:
1
:
1
in
Figure

4(c)
. According to the results in
Figure

4
, we can find that the average utility
\frac{\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}f_{\tau}(S_{\tau})}{t},\forall t\in[T]
of our proposed
Multinoulli-OSGA
and
Multinoulli-OSCG
algorithms can significantly exceed the state-of-the-art MA-OSMA and MA-OSEA algorithms in
(
zhang2025nearoptimal
)
as well as OSG in
(
xu2023online
)
. It is worth noting that as the proportion of ‘Adversarial’ targets increases, the maximum running average utility exhibits a downward trend for all curves.

VII-B
2
Tracking under Extended Kalman-Filter Framework

In previous section, we considered a simplified multi-target tracking model, that is, we assume that the monitoring quality of each moving target
j
only depends on its nearest UAV. However, in many real-world scenarios, relying
solely
on the information collected by the nearest UAV for accurately tracking the state of each target is unrealistic. Instead, we generally need to aggregate the data from multiple UAVs to comprehensively reconstruct the targets’ behaviors.

Recently, in order to obtain an accurate estimation of every target’s location,
(
hashemi2019submodular
;
hashemi2020randomized
)
employed an extend Kalman-filter(EKF) framework to process the observations of multiple distinct agents. Specifically, let us consider a target tracking task using a swarm of UAVs equipped with GPS and radar systems. In this scenario, at each time
t\in[T]
, each UAV
i
can measure the range to each target
j
throughout its radar system and also can obtain its own position information from GPS. Furthermore, like the work
(
hashemi2019submodular
)
, we also assume that the range measurements of the radar systems follow a quadratic model, that is to say,

r_{(\theta,s,i)\rightarrow j}(t)\triangleq\frac{1}{2}\|o_{(\theta,s,i)}(t)-o_{j}(t)\|^{2}_{2}+\xi_{(\theta,s,i)\rightarrow j}(t),

(9)

where the symbol
r_{(\theta,s,i)\rightarrow j}(t)
denotes the range measurement of UAV
i
to target
j
after UAV
i
executes the action
(\theta,s,i)
,
o_{j}(t)
is the location of target
j
,
\xi_{(\theta,s,i)\rightarrow j}(t)
is the additive independent noise and the symbol
o_{(\theta,s,i)}(t)
represents the new position of UAV
i
after moving from its previous location at time
t-1
with a movement angle
\theta
and speed
s
.

Note that when the new position
o_{(\theta,s,i)}(t)
and the range measurement
r_{(\theta,s,i)\rightarrow j}(t)
are known, we can view Eq.(
9
) as a random quadratic experiment of the unknown location
o_{j}(t)
. In other words, different action
(\theta,s,i)
can lead to distinct random quadratic observation of the unknown parameter
o_{j}(t)
. Inspired by this perspective, we can formulated the action selection problem in multi-target tracking task as an experimental design problem, which aims at selecting a feasible subset from the whole collection of experiments such that the measurements of these selected sub-experiments can accurately estimate the location of every target. Particularly, under the classical Van Trees’ inequality
(
van2004detection
)
, the work
(
hashemi2019submodular
)
established a lower bound for the covariance matrix associated with the EKF estimator of the location
o_{j}(t)
. Specifically, at time
t\in[T]
, if we consider the sub-experiment
S
, then the lower-bound matrix
\textbf{B}^{j}_{S}(t)
for the EKF estimate of the unknown location
o_{j}(t)
can be expressed as (Please refer to Theorem 2 in
(
hashemi2019submodular
)
):

\textbf{B}^{j}_{S}(t)\triangleq\left(\sum_{(\theta,s,i)\in S}\frac{\textbf{P}+\textbf{z}_{(\theta,s,i)\rightarrow j}\textbf{z}_{(\theta,s,i)\rightarrow j}^{T}}{\sigma^{2}_{(\theta,s,i)\rightarrow j}(t)}+\textbf{I}_{j}(t)\right)^{-1},

where
\sigma^{2}_{(\theta,s,i)\rightarrow j}(t)\triangleq\text{Var}\left(\xi_{(\theta,s,i)\rightarrow j}(t)\right)
,
\textbf{z}_{(\theta,s,i)\rightarrow j}\triangleq o_{(\theta,s,i)}(t)-o_{j}(t-1)-\mathbb{E}\left(o_{j}(t)-o_{j}(t-1)\right)
,
\textbf{P}\triangleq\text{Cov}\left(o_{j}(t)-o_{j}(t-1)\right)
and
\textbf{I}_{j}(t)
is the Fisher information matrix associated with the normalized random gap
\big(o_{j}(t)-o_{j}(t-1)-\mathbb{E}\left(o_{j}(t)-o_{j}(t-1)\right)\big)
.

With this lower-bound covariance matrix
\textbf{B}^{j}_{S}(t)
, we then can utilize various so-called alphabetical criteria
(
chaloner1995bayesian
)
to design a utility function for selecting a superior action set such that the resulting EKF estimation can accurately approximate the location of each target. One commonly used strategy is to employ the A-optimality, i.e., we consider minimizing the trace of the lower-bound covariance matrix
\textbf{B}^{j}_{S}(t)
or equivalently maximize
f_{t}(S)\triangleq\sum_{j=1}^{30}\left(\text{Tr}\big(\textbf{I}^{-1}_{j}(t)\big)-\text{Tr}\big(\textbf{B}^{j}_{S}(t)\big)\right)

where “
Tr
” is the trace of matrix and we consider
30
moving targets.

Thus, under the A-optimality criterion, we can model the UAVs’ action selection task as a special instance of
online
partition-constrained subset selection problem. Furthermore, according to recent studies
(
harshaw2019submodular
;
thiery2022two
)
, we can show that the A-optimal utility function is monotone
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular and
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular.

In our simulations, to simplify the computation, we model the movement of each target as a two-dimensional Brownian motion. Specifically, we set
o_{j}(t)\triangleq o_{j}(t-1)+0.02\cdot\mathcal{N}(\textbf{0}_{2},\textbf{I}_{2})
where
\textbf{I}_{2}
is the 2-dimensional identity matrix. Moreover, we assume that the noise
\xi_{(\theta,s,i)\rightarrow j}
follows an independent normal distribution, i.e.,
\xi_{(\theta,s,i)\rightarrow j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,0.01)
. As for UAVs, at every iteration
t\in[T]
, we adjust their speeds from a set of
2
,
7
, or
12
units/s and simultaneously change their movement directions from “up”, “down”, “left”, “right”, or “diagonally”. As a result, the action set
\mathcal{V}_{i}
available to each UAV
i\in[20]
can be mathematically formulated as:

\mathcal{V}_{i}\triangleq\left\{(\theta,s,i):s\in\{2,7,12\}\text{units/s},\theta\in\{\frac{\pi}{4},\frac{\pi}{2},\dots,2\pi\}\right\},

where
\theta
denotes the movement angle ,
s
is the speed and
i
represents the unique identifier.

Given the unknown DR ratio
\alpha
of our investigated set function
f_{t}
, in
Figure

4(e)
, we perform a brute-force
0.1
-network search to find the optimal parameter settings for
Multinoulli-OSGA
algorithm.
Subsequently, we compare the best
\alpha=0.1
case and the worse
\alpha=1
scenario with our proposed
Multinoulli-OSGA
and
Multinoulli-OSCG
algorithms in
Figure

4(d)
. According to the results in
Figure

4(d)
, we can find that the running average utility of our proposed
Multinoulli-OSGA
and
Multinoulli-OSCG
algorithms can significantly exceed the baseline ‘RANDOM’ algorithm, which is in accord with our theoretical findings. Moreover, we also find that the
parameter-free

Multinoulli-OSCG
algorithm can effectively outperform the
Multinoulli-OSCG
algorithm with a
0.1
-network search.
It is worth noting that no previous works explore the
online
subset selection problem with weakly submodular objectives. Thus, we adopt the ‘RANDOM’ algorithm as a baseline in
Figure

4(d)
. In ‘RANDOM’ baseline, we let each agent
i
randomly execute an action from its own action set
\mathcal{V}_{i}
at every time step
t\in[T]
.

VIII
Conclusion

This paper introduces a novel continuous-relaxation framework named Multinoulli Extension(
ME
) for the partition-constrained subset selection problem. In sharp contrast to the well-known multi-linear extension for submodular subset selection, a notable advantage of our
ME
is that it can provide a lossless round scheme for any set objective function. Subsequently, base on
ME
, we develop an efficient algorithm titled
Multinoulli-SCG
, which can achieve a value of
(1-e^{-\alpha})\text{OPT}-\epsilon
or
(\frac{\gamma^{2}(1-e^{-(\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2})})}{\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2}})\text{OPT}-\epsilon
with only
O(1/\epsilon^{2})
function evaluations for monotone
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular or
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular objective functions. This result significantly improves the previous
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/\epsilon^{6})
and
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/\epsilon^{3})
number of function evaluations associated with the distorted local-search methods.

Furthermore, based on our proposed
ME
, we also present two novel online algorithms, namely,
Multinoulli-OSCG
and
Multinoulli-OSGA
, for the unexplored
online
subset selection problems over partition constraints. Especially when each incoming set objective function is monotone
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular or
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular, we verify that both
Multinoulli-OSCG
and
Multinoulli-OSGA
can achieve a regret of
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{T})
against a
(1-e^{-\alpha})
-approximation or
\big(\frac{\gamma^{2}(1-e^{-(\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2})})}{\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2}}\big)
-approximation to the best feasible solution in hindsight, respectively. To the best of our knowledge, this is the
first
result to attain such approximation ratios with
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{T})
regret to the partition-constrained
online

\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular or
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular maximization problems. Finally,
extensive evaluations have been conducted to validate the effectiveness of our proposed algorithms.

a total of

at time

.

Appendix B
Additional Discussions

B-A

Multi-linear Extension

In this subsection, we introduce the multi-linear extension of
(
calinescu2011maximizing
)
for submodular maximization and simultaneously compare it with our proposed
M
ultinoulli
E
xtension(
ME
) in
Section

IV
. In order to better illustrate the multi-linear extension, this subsection supposes
|\mathcal{V}|=n
and decodes the ground set
\mathcal{V}
as
[n]
, i.e.,
\mathcal{V}\triangleq\{1,\dots,n\}
. Then, we can show that

Definition 3
.

For a set function
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we define its multi-linear extension as

G(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{V}}\Big(f(\mathcal{A})\prod_{a\in\mathcal{A}}x_{a}\prod_{a\notin\mathcal{A}}(1-x_{a})\Big)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{R}\sim\mathbf{x}}\Big(f(\mathcal{R})\Big),

(10)

where
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in[0,1]^{n}
and
\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
is a random set that contains each element
a\in\mathcal{V}
independently with probability
x_{a}
and excludes it with probability
1-x_{a}
. We write
\mathcal{R}\sim\mathbf{x}
to denote that
\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
is a random set sampled according to
\mathbf{x}
.

From the Eq.(
10
), we can view multi-linear extension
G
at any point
\mathbf{x}\in[0,1]^{n}
as the expected utility of independently selecting each action
a\in\mathcal{V}
with probability
x_{a}
. With this tool, we can cast the previous discrete subset selection problem (
1
) into a continuous maximization which learns the independent probability for each element
a\in\mathcal{V}
, that is, we consider the following continuous optimization:

\max_{\mathbf{x}\in[0,1]^{n}}G(\mathbf{x}),\ \ \text{ s.t.}\ \ \sum_{a\in\mathcal{V}_{k}}x_{a}\leq B_{k},\forall k\in[K]

(11)

where
G(\mathbf{x})
is the multi-linear extension of
f
.

It is important to note that, if we round any point
\mathbf{x}
included into the constraint of problem (
11
) throughout the definition of multi-linear extension, i.e., Eq.(
10
), there is a certain probability that the resulting subset will violate the partition constraint of the subset selection problem (
1
). Therefore, for multi-linear extension, we need to specifically design the rounding methods based on the properties of the set objective functions. However, current known lossless rounding schemes for multi-linear extension, such as pipage rounding
(
ageev2004pipage
)
, swap rounding
(
chekuri2010submodular
)
and contention resolution
(
chekuri2014submodular
)
, are heavily dependent on the
submodular
assumption. So far, how to losslessly round the multi-linear extension of
non-submodular
set functions, e.g.
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular and
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular functions, still remains an open question
(
thiery2022two
)
.

In contrast, our
ME
does not assign probabilities to any subsets that are out of the partition constraint of problem (
1
), which means that, for any set function
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
and any given
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\in\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
, we can, through the definition of
ME
, easily produce a subset that conforms to the partition constraint of problem (
1
) without any loss in terms of the expected function value
F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})
.

B-B

Non-closedness of the Domain
\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
about Coordinate-wise Maximum Operation

We need to emphasize that, when the set function
f
is monotone submodular,
Theorem

2
implies that our proposed
M
ultinoulli
E
xtension(
ME
) is also monotone continuous DR-submodular over the domain
\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
. However, it is worth noting that the former algorithms about continuous DR-submodular maximization
(
hassani2020stochastic
;
hassani2017gradient
;
zhang2022boosting
;
wan2023bandit
)
cannot be directly applied to our
ME
and the relaxed problem (
4
). This is because all of them highly rely on the following inequality: if
G
is a monotone continuous DR-submodular function,

\langle\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{x},\nabla G(\mathbf{x})\rangle\geq\langle\mathbf{y}\vee\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x},\nabla G(\mathbf{x})\rangle\geq G(\mathbf{y}\vee\mathbf{x})-G(\mathbf{x})\geq G(\mathbf{y})-G(\mathbf{x}),

(12)

where
\vee
is the coordinate-wise maximum operation, i.e.,
\mathbf{x}\vee\mathbf{y}\triangleq\max(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
.

Note that the second inequality in Eq.(
12
) requires that the vector
\mathbf{y}\vee\mathbf{x}
is included into the domain of objective function
G
. In other words, the domain of objective function
G
should be closed under the coordinate-wise maximum operation
\vee
. However, the domain
\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
of our
ME
does not meet this requirement. Similarly, to the best of our knowledge, the latest research on both upper-linearizable and weakly continuous DR-submodular maximization also requires the domain of the objective function to be closed under the coordinate-wise maximum operation
\vee
. (See
hassani2017gradient
;
zhang2022boosting
;
pedramfar2024from
)

Appendix C
The Fundamenta Properties of Multinoulli Extension

C-A

Proof of
Theorem

1

In this section, we prove the Theorem
1
.

Proof.

1):
At first, we review the definition of Multinoulli Extension. For any given set function
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
and any point
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\in\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
, we define its Multinoulli Extension for the subset selection problem (
1
) as:

F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}):=\sum_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\},\forall\hat{b}\in[B_{\hat{k}}],\forall\hat{k}\in[K]}\Big(f\big(\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{\hat{k}}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\big)\prod_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\prod_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{\hat{k}}}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Big),

(13)

where
\text{Pr}(v_{\hat{k}}^{m}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})=p^{m}_{\hat{k}}
and
\text{Pr}(\emptyset|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})=1-\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\hat{k}}}p_{\hat{k}}^{m}
for any
m\in[n_{\hat{k}}]
and
\hat{k}\in[K]
.

From Eq.(
13
), for any parameter
p^{m}_{k}
where
m\in[n_{k}]
and
k\in[K]
, we have the following equality:

\frac{\partial F}{\partial p^{m}_{k}}(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})=\sum_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\},\forall\hat{b}\in[B_{\hat{k}}],\forall\hat{k}\in[K]}\Big(f\big(\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{\hat{k}}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\big)\frac{\partial\Big(\prod_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\prod_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{\hat{k}}}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Big)}{\partial p^{m}_{k}}\Big).

(14)

Then, according to the definition of
\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})
, we can show that, if
k\neq\hat{k}
,
\frac{\partial\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})}{\partial p^{m}_{k}}=0
for any
e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\}
. When
k=\hat{k}
, we also can show that, if
e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}=v_{k}^{m}
,
\frac{\partial\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})}{\partial p^{m}_{k}}=1
and if
e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}=\emptyset
,
\frac{\partial\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})}{\partial p^{m}_{k}}=-1
. As for
e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\notin\{v_{k}^{m},\emptyset\}
, when
k=\hat{k}
,
\frac{\partial\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})}{\partial p^{m}_{k}}=0
. As a result, we can rewrite the Eq.(
14
) as:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial F}{\partial p^{m}_{k}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\},\forall\hat{b}\in[B_{\hat{k}}],\forall\hat{k}\in[K]}\Bigg(f\big(\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{\hat{k}}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\big)\sum_{\hat{b}_{1}\in[B_{k}]}\Bigg(\Big(\prod_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,\hat{b}_{1})}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Big)\frac{\partial\text{Pr}(e_{k}^{\hat{b}_{1}}|\mathbf{p}_{k})}{\partial p^{m}_{k}}\Bigg)\Bigg)

(15)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\hat{b}_{1}\in[B_{k}]}\Bigg(\sum_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\},\forall\hat{b}\in[B_{\hat{k}}],\forall\hat{k}\in[K]}\Big(f\big(\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{\hat{k}}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\big)\frac{\partial\text{Pr}(e_{k}^{\hat{b}_{1}}|\mathbf{p}_{k})}{\partial p^{m}_{k}}\Big(\prod_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,\hat{b}_{1})}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Big)\Big)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\hat{b}_{1}\in[B_{k}]}\Bigg(\sum_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\},(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,\hat{b}_{1})}\Bigg(\sum_{e_{k}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{k}\cup\{\emptyset\}}\Bigg(f\big(\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{\hat{k}}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\big)\frac{\partial\text{Pr}(e_{k}^{\hat{b}_{1}}|\mathbf{p}_{k})}{\partial p^{m}_{k}}\prod_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,\hat{b}_{1})}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Bigg)\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\hat{b}_{1}\in[B_{k}]}\Bigg(\sum_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\},(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,\hat{b}_{1})}\Bigg(f\Big(v_{k}^{m}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,\hat{b}_{1})}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\prod_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,\hat{b}_{1})}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=B_{k}\Bigg(\sum_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\},(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\Bigg(f\Big(v_{k}^{m}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\prod_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=B_{k}\Bigg(\Big(\sum_{e_{k}^{1}\in\mathcal{V}_{k}\cup\{\emptyset\}}\text{Pr}(e_{k}^{1}|\mathbf{p}_{k})\Big)\sum_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\},(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\Bigg(f\Big(v_{k}^{m}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\prod_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=B_{k}\Bigg(\sum_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\},\forall\hat{b}\in[B_{\hat{k}}],\forall\hat{k}\in[K]}\Bigg(f\Big(v_{k}^{m}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\prod_{\hat{k}\in[K]}\prod_{\hat{b}\in[B_{k}]}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=B_{k}\Bigg(\mathbb{E}_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\sim\emph{Multi($\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}$)}}\Big(f\Big(v_{k}^{m}\Big|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\Big)\Bigg),

where the fifth equality comes from that each random element
e_{k}^{\hat{b}_{1}}
is independently drawn the same multinoulli distribution Multi(
\mathbf{p}_{k}
) for any
\hat{b}_{1}\in[B_{k}]
and the sixth equality follows from
\sum_{e_{k}^{1}\in\mathcal{V}_{k}\cup\{\emptyset\}}\text{Pr}(e_{k}^{1}|\mathbf{p}_{k})=1
.

2):
Next, we prove the second point of Theorem
1
. At first, the monotonicity of
f
implies that for any two subsets
A\subseteq B\subseteq\mathcal{V}
,
f(A)\leq f(B)
such that we know that
f\Big(v_{k}^{m}\Big|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\geq 0
for any random elements
e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\forall\hat{b}\in[B_{\hat{k}}],\forall\hat{k}\in[K]
. As a result, we have If
f
is monotone, then
\frac{\partial F}{\partial p_{k}^{m}}\geq 0,\forall k\in[K],m\in[n_{k}]
.

3):
As for the third point, we firstly unify the process of generating random elements regarding two different multinoulli distributions with parameters
\mathbf{p}_{k}=(p_{k}^{1},\dots,p_{k}^{n_{k}})\in\Delta_{n_{k}}
and
\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k}=(\hat{p}_{k}^{1},\dots,\hat{p}_{k}^{n_{k}})\in\Delta_{n_{k}}
where
\hat{p}_{k}^{m}\geq p_{k}^{m}
for any
m\in[n_{k}]
and
k\in[K]
. More specifically, we will transform the sampling process from each multinoulli distribution Multi(
\mathbf{p}_{k}
) or Multi(
\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k}
)
into a function of two independent uniform random variables on the interval
[0,1]
. Namely, for any two independent uniform random variables
X,Y
on the interval
[0,1]
, we define that

e(X,Y,\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})=\left\{\begin{aligned} &v_{k}^{1}\ \ \ \text{If}\ X\in[0,p_{k}^{1})\\
&v_{k}^{c}\ \ \ \text{If}\ X\in[\sum_{m=1}^{c-1}p_{k}^{m},\sum_{m=1}^{c}p_{k}^{m})\ \text{for some integer}\ c\in[2,n_{k}]\\
&\emptyset\ \ \ \ \ \text{If}\ X\geq\sum_{m=1}^{n_{k}}p_{k}^{m}\end{aligned}\right.

We can easily check that, when
X
and
Y
are uniform random variables over the interval
[0,1]
, the random element
e(X,Y,\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})

follows the multinoulli distribution Multi(
\mathbf{p}_{k}
) over the community
\mathcal{V}_{k}:=\{v_{k}^{1},\dots,v_{k}^{n_{k}}\}
, where
\mathbf{p}_{k}=(p_{k}^{1},\dots,p_{k}^{n_{k}})\in\Delta_{n_{k}}
and
\text{Pr}\big(e(X,Y,\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})=v_{k}^{m}\big)=p_{k}^{m}
. Similarly, we also can generate a random element
\widehat{e}
from the community
\mathcal{V}_{k}
according to the multinoulli distribution Multi(
\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k}
) if we set

\widehat{e}(X,Y,\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})=\left\{\begin{aligned} &e\ \ \ \ \ \ \text{If}\ X<\sum_{m=1}^{n_{k}}p_{k}^{m}\\
&v_{k}^{1}\ \ \ \ \text{If}\ X\geq\sum_{m=1}^{n_{k}}p_{k}^{m}\ \text{and}\ Y\in[0,\frac{\hat{p}_{k}^{1}-p_{k}^{1}}{1-\sum_{m=1}^{n_{k}}p_{k}^{m}})\\
&v_{k}^{c}\ \ \ \ \text{If}\ X\geq\sum_{m=1}^{n_{k}}p_{k}^{m}\ \text{and}\ Y\in\Big[\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{c-1}(\hat{p}_{k}^{m}-p_{k}^{m})}{1-\sum_{m=1}^{n_{k}}p_{k}^{m}},\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{c}(\hat{p}_{k}^{m}-p_{k}^{m})}{1-\sum_{m=1}^{n_{k}}p_{k}^{m}}\Big)\ \text{for}\ c\in[2,n_{k}]\\
&\emptyset\ \ \ \ \ \ \text{If}\ X\geq\sum_{m=1}^{n_{k}}p_{k}^{m}\ \text{and}\ Y\geq\sum_{m=1}^{n_{k}}(\hat{p}_{k}^{m}-p_{k}^{m})\end{aligned}\right.

From the definition of
e(X,Y,\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})
and
\widehat{e}(X,Y,\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})
, we can show that, for any fixed
X,Y
,
\{e(X,Y,\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\subseteq\{\widehat{e}(X,Y,\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}
. Thus, if we generate multiple independent pairs
(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}})
for any
\hat{b}\in[B_{\hat{k}}]
and
\hat{k}\in[K]
, from the first point of Theorem
1
, we have that,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial F}{\partial p_{k}^{m}}(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})=B_{k}\Bigg(\mathbb{E}\Big(f\Big(v_{k}^{m}\Big|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\Big)\Big)\Bigg)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial F}{\partial p_{k}^{m}}(\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\dots,\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{K})=B_{k}\Bigg(\mathbb{E}\Big(f\Big(v_{k}^{m}\Big|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{\widehat{e}_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\Big)\Big)\Bigg).

Due to
\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\subseteq\{\widehat{e}_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}
, we can show that,

\Big(\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\Big)\subseteq\Big(\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{\widehat{e}_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\Big).

As a result, from the definition of weakly DR-submodularity, we know that if
f
is
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular, we have
\frac{\partial F}{\partial p_{k}^{m}}(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\geq\alpha\frac{\partial F}{\partial p_{k}^{m}}(\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\dots,\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{K})
such that
\nabla F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\geq\alpha\nabla F(\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\dots,\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{K})
.

4):
We prove the fourth point of Theorem
1
. From the definition of Multinoulli Extension and previous definitions of
e(X,Y,\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})
and
\widehat{e}(X,Y,\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})
, we can infer that the Multinoulli Extension
F
of set function
f
satisfies the following relationships:

\displaystyle F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})=\mathbb{E}\Bigg(f\Big(\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{k}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\Big)\Bigg)

\displaystyle F(\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\dots,\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{K})=\mathbb{E}\Bigg(f\Big(\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{k}}\{\widehat{e}_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\Big)\Bigg).

As a result, we have

F(\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\dots,\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{K})-F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})=\mathbb{E}\Bigg(f\Big(\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{k}}\{\widehat{e}_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\Big)-f\Big(\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{k}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\Big)\Bigg).

If
f
is
\gamma
-weakly submodular from below and
\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k}\geq\mathbf{p}_{k}
for any
k\in[K]
, we have that

\displaystyle\gamma\Big(F(\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\dots,\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{K})-F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\Big)

\displaystyle=\gamma\mathbb{E}\Bigg(f\Big(\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{k}}\{\widehat{e}_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\Big)-f\Big(\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{k}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\Big)\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\bar{k}=1}^{K}\sum_{\bar{b}=1}^{B_{\bar{k}}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg(f\Big(\widehat{e}_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}(X_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},Y_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},\mathbf{p}_{\bar{k}},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{\bar{k}})\Big|\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{k}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\Big)\Bigg),

where the final inequality follows from the
\gamma
-weakly submodular property of
f
, namely, for any two subsets
A\subseteq B\subseteq\mathcal{V}
,
\sum_{v\in B}f(v|A)=\sum_{v\in B\setminus A}f(v|A)\geq\gamma\Big(f(B)-f(A)\Big)
.

Then, if
X_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}<\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\bar{k}}}p_{\bar{k}}^{m}
, we know that
\widehat{e}_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}(X_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},Y_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},\mathbf{p}_{\bar{k}},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{\bar{k}})=e_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}(X_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},Y_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},\mathbf{p}_{\bar{k}},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{\bar{k}})
such that
f\Big(\widehat{e}_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}(X_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},Y_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},\mathbf{p}_{\bar{k}},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{\bar{k}})\Big|\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{k}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\Big)=0
. As for the case that
X_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}\geq\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\bar{k}}}p_{\bar{k}}^{m}
, namely,
e_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}(X_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},Y_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},\mathbf{p}_{\bar{k}},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{\bar{k}})=\emptyset
, which means that we have the following equality:

\displaystyle f\Big(\widehat{e}_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}(X_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},Y_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},\mathbf{p}_{\bar{k}},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{\bar{k}})\Big|\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{k}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\Big)

(16)

\displaystyle=f\Big(\widehat{e}_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}(X_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},Y_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},\mathbf{p}_{\bar{k}},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{\bar{k}})\Big|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(\bar{k},\bar{b})}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\Big).

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\gamma\Big(F(\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\dots,\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{K})-F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\bar{k}=1}^{K}\sum_{\bar{b}=1}^{B_{\bar{k}}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg(f\Big(\widehat{e}_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}(X_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},Y_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},\mathbf{p}_{\bar{k}},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{\bar{k}})\Big|\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{k}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\Big)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bar{k}=1}^{K}\sum_{\bar{b}=1}^{B_{\bar{k}}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg(\mathbb{E}\Bigg(f\Big(\widehat{e}_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}(X_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},Y_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},\mathbf{p}_{\bar{k}},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{\bar{k}})\Big|\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{k}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\Big)\Bigg|(X_{k}^{b},Y_{k}^{b}),(k,b)\neq(\bar{k},\bar{b})\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bar{k}=1}^{K}\sum_{\bar{b}=1}^{B_{\bar{k}}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg(\mathbb{E}\Bigg(f\Big(\widehat{e}_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}(X_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},Y_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},\mathbf{p}_{\bar{k}},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{\bar{k}})\Big|\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{k}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\Big)I(X_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}<\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\bar{k}}}p_{\bar{k}}^{m})\Bigg|(X_{k}^{b},Y_{k}^{b}),(k,b)\neq(\bar{k},\bar{b})\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\bar{k}=1}^{K}\sum_{\bar{b}=1}^{B_{\bar{k}}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg(\mathbb{E}\Bigg(f\Big(\widehat{e}_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}(X_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},Y_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},\mathbf{p}_{\bar{k}},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{\bar{k}})\Big|\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{k}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\Big)I(X_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}\geq\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\bar{k}}}p_{\bar{k}}^{m})\Bigg|(X_{k}^{b},Y_{k}^{b}),(k,b)\neq(\bar{k},\bar{b})\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bar{k}=1}^{K}\sum_{\bar{b}=1}^{B_{\bar{k}}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg(\mathbb{E}\Bigg(f\Big(\widehat{e}_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}(X_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},Y_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},\mathbf{p}_{\bar{k}},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{\bar{k}})\Big|\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{k}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\Big)I(X_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}\geq\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\bar{k}}}p_{\bar{k}}^{m})\Bigg|(X_{k}^{b},Y_{k}^{b}),(k,b)\neq(\bar{k},\bar{b})\Bigg)\Bigg),

where the final equality comes from
f\Big(\widehat{e}_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}(X_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},Y_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},\mathbf{p}_{\bar{k}},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{\bar{k}})\Big|\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{k}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\Big)=0
when
X_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}<\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\bar{k}}}p_{\bar{k}}^{m}
.
∎

Then, from Eq.(
16
), we also can show that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg(f\Big(\widehat{e}_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}(X_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},Y_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},\mathbf{p}_{\bar{k}},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{\bar{k}})\Big|\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{k}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\Big)I(X_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}\geq\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\bar{k}}}p_{\bar{k}}^{m})\Bigg|(X_{k}^{b},Y_{k}^{b}),(k,b)\neq(\bar{k},\bar{b})\Bigg)

(17)

\displaystyle=\sum_{c=1}^{n_{\bar{k}}}\text{Pr}\Big(\widehat{e}_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}(X_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},Y_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},\mathbf{p}_{\bar{k}},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{\bar{k}})=v_{\bar{k}}^{c},X_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}\geq\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\bar{k}}}p_{\bar{k}}^{m}\Big)f\Big(v_{\bar{k}}^{c}\Big|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(\bar{k},\bar{b})}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{c=1}^{n_{\bar{k}}}(\hat{p}^{c}_{\bar{k}}-p^{c}_{\bar{k}})f\Big(v_{\bar{k}}^{c}\Big|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(\bar{k},\bar{b})}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\Big),

where the equality comes from that

\displaystyle\text{Pr}\Big(\widehat{e}_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}(X_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},Y_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}},\mathbf{p}_{\bar{k}},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{\bar{k}})=v_{\bar{k}}^{c},X_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}\geq\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\bar{k}}}p_{\bar{k}}^{m}\Big)

\displaystyle=\text{Pr}\Big(X_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}\geq\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\bar{k}}}p_{\bar{k}}^{m},Y_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}\in\Big[\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{c-1}(\hat{p}_{\bar{k}}^{m}-p_{\bar{k}}^{m})}{1-\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\bar{k}}}p_{\bar{k}}^{m}},\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{c}(\hat{p}_{\bar{k}}^{m}-p_{\bar{k}}^{m})}{1-\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\bar{k}}}p_{\bar{k}}^{m}}\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle=(\hat{p}^{m}_{\bar{k}}-p^{m}_{\bar{k}}).

As a result, we have

\displaystyle\gamma\Big(F(\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\dots,\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{K})-F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bar{k}=1}^{K}\sum_{\bar{b}=1}^{B_{\bar{k}}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg(\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\bar{k}}}(\hat{p}^{m}_{\bar{k}}-p^{m}_{\bar{k}})f\Big(v_{\bar{k}}^{m}\Big|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(\bar{k},\bar{b})}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\Big)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bar{k}=1}^{K}\sum_{\bar{b}=1}^{B_{\bar{k}}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg(\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\bar{k}}}(\hat{p}^{m}_{\bar{k}}-p^{m}_{\bar{k}})f\Big(v_{\bar{k}}^{m}\Big|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(\bar{k},1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\Big)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bar{k}=1}^{K}\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\bar{k}}}(\hat{p}^{m}_{\bar{k}}-p^{m}_{\bar{k}})B_{\bar{k}}\mathbb{E}\Bigg(f\Big(v_{\bar{k}}^{m}\Big|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(\bar{k},1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}(X_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},Y_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}},\mathbf{p}_{k},\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{k})\}\Big)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bar{k}=1}^{K}\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\bar{k}}}(\hat{p}^{m}_{\bar{k}}-p^{m}_{\bar{k}})\frac{\partial F}{\partial p^{m}_{\bar{k}}}(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})=\Big\langle(\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{1},\dots,\hat{\mathbf{p}}_{K})-(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}),\nabla F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\Big\rangle,

where the second equality follows from the independence of
e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}
for any
\hat{b}\in[B_{\hat{k}}]
and
k\in[K]
.

C-B

Proof of
Theorem

2

In this section, we verify the Theorem
2
.

Proof.

At first, we recall that
\mathcal{V}_{k}:=\{v_{k}^{1},\dots,v_{k}^{n_{k}}\}
for any
k\in[K]
. Therefore, for any subset
S
within the partition constraint of problem (
1
), we assume
|S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k}|=s_{k}\leq B_{k}
and we can represent each
S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k}
as

S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k}=\{v^{m_{k}^{1}}_{k},\dots,v^{m_{k}^{s_{k}}}_{k}\},

where
m_{k}^{b_{1}}\neq m_{k}^{b_{2}}\in[n_{k}]
for any
b_{1},b_{2}\in[s_{k}]
for any
k\in[K]
.

As a result, we can rewrite that

\displaystyle\left\langle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\mathbf{1}_{S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k}}}{B_{k}},\nabla F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\right\rangle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{b=1}^{s_{k}}\frac{1}{B_{k}}\frac{\partial F}{\partial p_{k}^{m_{k}^{b}}}(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})

(18)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{b=1}^{s_{k}}\Bigg(\mathbb{E}_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\sim\emph{Multi($\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}$)}}\Big(f\Big(v_{k}^{m_{k}^{b}}\Big|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\Big)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{b=1}^{s_{k}}\Bigg(\mathbb{E}_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\sim\emph{Multi($\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}$)}}\Big(f\Big(v_{k}^{m_{k}^{b}}\Big|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,b)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\Big)\Bigg),

where the final equality follows from that, for any fixed
\hat{k}\in[K]
, the elements
e_{\hat{k}}^{b},\forall b\in[B_{\hat{k}}]
are independently drawn from the same multinoulli distribution Multi(
\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}
).

When the set function
f
is
\alpha
-weakly monotone DR-submodular, we can show that

\displaystyle\left\langle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\mathbf{1}_{S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k}}}{B_{k}},\nabla F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\right\rangle

(19)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{b=1}^{s_{k}}\Bigg(\mathbb{E}_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\sim\emph{Multi($\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}$)}}\Big(f\Big(v_{k}^{m_{k}^{b}}\Big|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,b)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\Big)\Bigg)

\displaystyle\geq\alpha\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{b=1}^{s_{k}}\Bigg(\mathbb{E}_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\sim\emph{Multi($\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}$)}}\Big(f\Big(v_{k}^{m_{k}^{b}}\Big|\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{\hat{k}}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\bigcup\big(\cup_{(\bar{k},\bar{b})<(k,b)}\{v_{\bar{k}}^{m_{\bar{k}}^{\bar{b}}}\}\big)\Big)\Big)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\alpha\Bigg(\mathbb{E}_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\sim\emph{Multi($\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}$)}}\Big(f\Big(\cup_{k=1}^{K}\cup_{b=1}^{s_{k}}\{v_{k}^{m_{k}^{b}}\}\Big|\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{\hat{k}}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\Big)\Bigg)

\displaystyle\geq\alpha\Bigg(\mathbb{E}_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\sim\emph{Multi($\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}$)}}\Big(f\Big(\cup_{k=1}^{K}\cup_{b=1}^{s_{k}}\{v_{k}^{m_{k}^{b}}\}\Big)-f\Big(\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{\hat{k}}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\Big)\Bigg)\ \ \ \text{(Monotonicity)}

\displaystyle=\alpha\Big(f(S)-F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\Big),

where the first inequality follows from the
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodularity, namely,
f(v|A)\geq\alpha f(v|B)
for any two subsets
A\subseteq B\subseteq\mathcal{V}
and the partially ordered set
\{(\bar{k},\bar{b})<(k,b)\}=\{(\bar{k},\bar{b})\big|\bar{k}<k\ \text{or}\ \bar{b}<b\ \text{when}\ \bar{k}=k\}
.

As for the settings that the set function
f
is
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly monotone submodular, we firstly can show that for any two elements
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathcal{V}
and the subset
B\subseteq\mathcal{V}
, we have that

\gamma\big(f(e_{1}|B\cup\{e_{2}\})+f(e_{2}|B)\big)=\gamma\big(f(B\cup\{e_{1},e_{2}\})-f(B)\big)\leq f(e_{1}|B)+f(e_{2}|B),

such that

f(e_{1}|B)\geq\gamma f(e_{1}|B\cup\{e_{2}\})-(1-\gamma)f(e_{2}|B).

(20)

From Eq.(
20
), we can show that

\displaystyle\left\langle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\mathbf{1}_{S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k}}}{B_{k}},\nabla F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\right\rangle

(21)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{b=1}^{s_{k}}\Bigg(\mathbb{E}_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\sim\emph{Multi($\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}$)}}\Big(f\Big(v_{k}^{m_{k}^{b}}\Big|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,b)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\Big)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{b=1}^{s_{k}}\Bigg(\mathbb{E}_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\sim\emph{Multi($\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}$)}}\Big(\gamma f\Big(v_{k}^{m_{k}^{b}}\Big|\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{\hat{k}}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)-(1-\gamma)f\Big(e_{k}^{b}\Big|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,b)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\Big)\Big)\Bigg)

Then, from the
\gamma
-weakly submodularity,

\displaystyle\Bigg(\mathbb{E}_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\sim\emph{Multi($\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}$)}}\Big(f\Big(v_{k}^{m_{k}^{b}}\Big|\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{\hat{k}}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\Big)\Big)\Bigg)

(22)

\displaystyle\geq\gamma\mathbb{E}_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\sim\emph{Multi($\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}$)}}\Big(f\Big(\cup_{k=1}^{K}\cup_{b=1}^{s_{k}}\{v_{k}^{m_{k}^{b}}\}\Big|\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{\hat{k}}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle\geq\gamma\Big(f(S)-F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\Big),

where the final inequality follows from the monotonicity.

Furthermore, from the
\beta
-weakly upper submodularity,we also have,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{b=1}^{s_{k}}\Bigg(\mathbb{E}_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\sim\emph{Multi($\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}$)}}\Big(f\Big(e_{k}^{b}\Big|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,b)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\Big)\Big)\Bigg)

(23)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{b=1}^{B_{k}}\Bigg(\mathbb{E}_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\sim\emph{Multi($\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}$)}}\Big(f\Big(e_{k}^{b}\Big|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,b)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\Big)\Big)\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\beta\mathbb{E}_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\sim\emph{Multi($\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}$)}}\Big(f\Big(\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{\hat{k}}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)-f(\emptyset)\Big)\leq\beta F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}),

where the second inequality follows from the definition of
\beta
-upper submodularity, namely, Eq.(
3
).

Merging Eq.(
23
) and Eq.(
22
) into Eq.(
21
), we have that

\displaystyle\left\langle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\mathbf{1}_{S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k}}}{B_{k}},\nabla F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{b=1}^{s_{k}}\Bigg(\mathbb{E}_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\sim\emph{Multi($\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}$)}}\Big(\gamma f\Big(v_{k}^{m_{k}^{b}}\Big|\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{\hat{k}}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)-(1-\gamma)f\Big(e_{k}^{b}\Big|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,b)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\Big)\Big)\Bigg)

\displaystyle\geq\gamma^{2}(f(S)-F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}))-(1-\gamma)\beta F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})=\gamma^{2}f(S)-(\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2})F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}).

∎

C-C

Proof of
Theorem

6

In this section, we verify the Theorem
6
.

Proof.

Firstly, from the fifth equality in Eq.(
15
), we know that

\frac{\partial F}{\partial p^{m}_{k}}(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})=B_{k}\Bigg(\sum_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\},(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\Bigg(f\Big(v_{k}^{m}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\prod_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Bigg)\Bigg).

Therefore, if
k_{1}\neq k_{2}\in[K]
, for any
m_{1}\in[n_{k_{1}}]
and
m_{2}\in[n_{k_{2}}]
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial p_{k_{1}}^{m_{1}}\partial p_{k_{2}}^{m_{2}}}(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})

\displaystyle=B_{k_{1}}\Bigg(\sum_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\},(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k_{1},1)}\Bigg(f\Big(v_{k_{1}}^{m_{1}}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k_{1},1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\frac{\partial\Big(\prod_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k_{1},1)}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Big)}{\partial p_{k_{2}}^{m_{2}}}\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=B_{k_{1}}\Bigg(\sum_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\},(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k_{1},1)}\Bigg(f\Big(v_{k_{1}}^{m_{1}}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k_{1},1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\sum_{\hat{b}_{1}\in[B_{k_{2}}]}\frac{\partial\text{Pr}(e_{k_{2}}^{\hat{b}_{1}}|\mathbf{p}_{k_{2}})}{\partial p^{m_{2}}_{k_{2}}}\Big(\prod_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\{(k_{1},1),(k_{2},\hat{b}_{1})\}}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Big)\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=B_{k_{1}}\sum_{\hat{b}_{1}\in[B_{k_{2}}]}\Bigg(\sum_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\},(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k_{1},1)}\Bigg(f\Big(v_{k_{1}}^{m_{1}}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k_{1},1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\frac{\partial\text{Pr}(e_{k_{2}}^{\hat{b}_{1}}|\mathbf{p}_{k_{2}})}{\partial p^{m_{2}}_{k_{2}}}\Big(\prod_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\{(k_{1},1),(k_{2},\hat{b}_{1})\}}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Big)\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=B_{k_{1}}B_{k_{2}}\Bigg(\sum_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\left\{(k_{1},1),(k_{2},1)\right\}}\Bigg(\sum_{e_{k_{2}}^{1}\in\mathcal{V}_{k_{2}}\cup\{\emptyset\}}f\Big(v_{k_{1}}^{m_{1}}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k_{1},1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\frac{\partial\text{Pr}(e_{k_{2}}^{1}|\mathbf{p}_{k_{2}})}{\partial p^{m_{2}}_{k_{2}}}\Big(\prod_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\{(k_{1},1),(k_{2},1)\}}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Big)\Bigg)\Bigg).

Due to that
\text{Pr}(v_{\hat{k}}^{m}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})=p^{m}_{\hat{k}}
and
\text{Pr}(\emptyset|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})=1-\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\hat{k}}}p_{\hat{k}}^{m}
, we can show that

\displaystyle\sum_{e_{k_{2}}^{1}\in\mathcal{V}_{k_{2}}\cup\{\emptyset\}}f\Big(v_{k_{1}}^{m_{1}}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k_{1},1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\frac{\partial\text{Pr}(e_{k_{2}}^{1}|\mathbf{p}_{k_{2}})}{\partial p^{m_{2}}_{k_{2}}}

\displaystyle=f\Big(v_{k_{1}}^{m_{1}}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\{(k_{1},1),(k_{2},1)\}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\bigcup\{v_{k_{2}}^{m_{2}}\}\Big)-f\Big(v_{k_{1}}^{m_{1}}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\{(k_{1},1),(k_{2},1)\}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big).

Therefore, if we set
S=\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\left\{(k_{1},1),(k_{2},1)\right\}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}
, we can show that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial p_{k_{1}}^{m_{1}}\partial p_{k_{2}}^{m_{2}}}(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})

\displaystyle=B_{k_{1}}B_{k_{2}}\Bigg(\sum_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\left\{(k_{1},1),(k_{2},1)\right\}}\Bigg(\sum_{e_{k_{2}}^{1}\in\mathcal{V}_{k_{2}}\cup\{\emptyset\}}f\Big(v_{k_{1}}^{m_{1}}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k_{1},1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\frac{\partial\text{Pr}(e_{k_{2}}^{1}|\mathbf{p}_{k_{2}})}{\partial p^{m_{2}}_{k_{2}}}\Big(\prod_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\{(k_{1},1),(k_{2},1)\}}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Big)\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=B_{k_{1}}B_{k_{2}}\Bigg(\sum_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\},(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\left\{(k_{1},1),(k_{2},1)\right\}}\Bigg(f\Big(v_{k_{1}}^{m_{1}}|S\cup\{v_{k_{2}}^{m_{2}}\}\Big)-f\Big(v_{k_{1}}^{m_{1}}|S\Big)\Big(\prod_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\{(k_{1},1),(k_{2},1)\}}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Big)\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=B_{k_{1}}B_{k_{2}}\mathbb{E}_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\sim\text{Multi}(\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})}\Bigg(f\Big(v_{k_{1}}^{m_{1}}|S\cup\{v_{k_{2}}^{m_{2}}\}\Big)-f\Big(v_{k_{1}}^{m_{1}}|S\Big)\Bigg),

where the final equality comes from that the subset
S=\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\left\{(k_{1},1),(k_{2},1)\right\}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}
is unrelated with the random elements
e_{k_{1}}^{1}
and
e_{k_{2}}^{1}
.

When
k_{1}=k_{2}=k\in[K]
and
B_{k}=1
, it is easy to verify that
\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial p_{k}^{m_{1}}\partial p_{k}^{m_{2}}}(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})=0
. As for
B_{k}\geq 2
, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial p_{k}^{m_{1}}\partial p_{k}^{m_{2}}}(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})

\displaystyle=B_{k}\Bigg(\sum_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\},(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\Bigg(f\Big(v_{k}^{m_{1}}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\frac{\partial\Big(\prod_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Big)}{\partial p_{k}^{m_{2}}}\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=B_{k}\Bigg(\sum_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\},(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\Bigg(f\Big(v_{k}^{m_{1}}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\sum_{2\leq\hat{b}_{1}\leq B_{k}}\frac{\partial\text{Pr}(e_{k}^{\hat{b}_{1}}|\mathbf{p}_{k})}{\partial p^{m_{2}}_{k}}\Big(\prod_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\{(k,1),(k,\hat{b}_{1})\}}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Big)\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=B_{k}\sum_{2\leq\hat{b}_{1}\leq B_{k}}\Bigg(\sum_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\},(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\Bigg(f\Big(v_{k}^{m_{1}}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\frac{\partial\text{Pr}(e_{k}^{\hat{b}_{1}}|\mathbf{p}_{k})}{\partial p^{m_{2}}_{k}}\Big(\prod_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\{(k,1),(k,\hat{b}_{1})\}}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Big)\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=B_{k}(B_{k}-1)\Bigg(\sum_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\},(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\Bigg(f\Big(v_{k}^{m_{1}}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\frac{\partial\text{Pr}(e_{k}^{2}|\mathbf{p}_{k})}{\partial p^{m_{2}}_{k}}\Big(\prod_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\{(k,1),(k,2)\}}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Big)\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=B_{k}(B_{k}-1)\Bigg(\sum_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\{(k,1),(k,2)\}}\Bigg(\sum_{e_{k}^{2}\in\mathcal{V}_{k}\cup\{\emptyset\}}f\Big(v_{k}^{m_{1}}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\frac{\partial\text{Pr}(e_{k}^{2}|\mathbf{p}_{k})}{\partial p^{m_{2}}_{k}}\Big(\prod_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\{(k,1),(k,2)\}}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Big)\Bigg)\Bigg).

Also, because
\text{Pr}(v_{\hat{k}}^{m}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})=p^{m}_{\hat{k}}
and
\text{Pr}(\emptyset|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})=1-\sum_{m=1}^{n_{\hat{k}}}p_{\hat{k}}^{m}
, we can show that

\displaystyle\sum_{e_{k}^{2}\in\mathcal{V}_{k}\cup\{\emptyset\}}f\Big(v_{k}^{m_{1}}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\frac{\partial\text{Pr}(e_{k}^{2}|\mathbf{p}_{k})}{\partial p^{m_{2}}_{k_{2}}}

\displaystyle=f\Big(v_{k}^{m_{1}}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\{(k,1),(k,2)\}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\bigcup\{v_{k}^{m_{2}}\}\Big)-f\Big(v_{k}^{m_{1}}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\{(k,1),(k,2)\}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big).

As a result, if we set
S=\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\left\{(k,1),(k,2)\right\}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}
, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial p_{k}^{m_{1}}\partial p_{k}^{m_{2}}}(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})

\displaystyle=B_{k}(B_{k}-1)\Bigg(\sum_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\},(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\{(k,1),(k,2)\}}\Bigg(\Big(f\Big(v_{k}^{m_{1}}|S\cup\{v_{k}^{m_{2}}\}\Big)-f\Big(v_{k}^{m_{1}}|S\Big)\Big)\Big(\prod_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\{(k,1),(k,2)\}}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Big)\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(B_{k}^{2}-B_{k})\mathbb{E}_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\sim\text{Multi}(\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})}\Bigg(f\Big(v_{k}^{m_{1}}|S\cup\{v_{k}^{m_{2}}\}\Big)-f\Big(v_{k}^{m_{1}}|S\Big)\Bigg),

where the final equality comes from that the subset
S=\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq\left\{(k,1),(k,2)\right\}}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}
is unrelated with the random elements
e_{k}^{1}
and
e_{k}^{2}
.∎

Appendix D
Stationary Point and Non-oblivious Auxiliary Functions

D-A

Proof of
Theorem

3

In this section, we prove the Theorem
3
.
Firstly, we prove a lower bound about
\Big\langle(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}),\nabla F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\Big\rangle
for any point
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\in\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
, that is to say,

Theorem 10
.

When the set function
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular, for any point
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\in\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
, the following inequality holds:

\Big\langle(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}),\nabla F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\Big\rangle\geq\frac{1}{\alpha}F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}),

(24)

where
\alpha\in(0,1]
. Similarly, when the set function
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is
\beta
-weakly submodular from above, for any point
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\in\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
, we also can infer that

\Big\langle(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}),\nabla F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\Big\rangle\geq\beta F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}),

where
\beta\geq 1
.

Proof.

At first, we assume each
\mathbf{p}_{k}:=(p_{k}^{1},\dots,p_{k}^{n_{k}})
for any
k\in[K]
. Then, from the first point of Theorem
1
and the fifth equality in Eq.(
15
), we have that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial F}{\partial p_{k}^{m}}(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})

\displaystyle=B_{k}\Bigg(\mathbb{E}_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\sim\emph{Multi($\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}$)}}\Big(f\Big(v_{k}^{m}\Big|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\Big)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=B_{k}\Bigg(\sum_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\},(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\Bigg(f\Big(v_{k}^{m}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\prod_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Bigg)\Bigg)

As a result, we have that

\displaystyle\Big\langle(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}),\nabla F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{m=1}^{n_{k}}p_{k}^{m}\frac{\partial F}{\partial p_{k}^{m}}(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{m=1}^{n_{k}}p_{k}^{m}B_{k}\Bigg(\mathbb{E}_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\sim\emph{Multi($\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}$)}}\Big(f\Big(v_{k}^{m}\Big|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\Big)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{m=1}^{n_{k}}p_{k}^{m}B_{k}\Bigg(\sum_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\},(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\Bigg(f\Big(v_{k}^{m}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\prod_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{m=1}^{n_{k}}\text{Pr}(v_{k}^{m}|\mathbf{p}_{k})B_{k}\Bigg(\sum_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\},(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\Bigg(f\Big(v_{k}^{m}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\prod_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}B_{k}\sum_{m=1}^{n_{k}}\text{Pr}(v_{k}^{m}|\mathbf{p}_{k})\Bigg(\sum_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\},(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\Bigg(f\Big(v_{k}^{m}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\prod_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}B_{k}\Bigg(\sum_{m=1}^{n_{k}}\sum_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\},(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\Bigg(f\Big(v_{k}^{m}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\text{Pr}(v_{k}^{m}|\mathbf{p}_{k})\prod_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}B_{k}\Bigg(\sum_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\in\mathcal{V}_{\hat{k}}\cup\{\emptyset\},\forall\hat{b}\in[B_{\hat{k}}],\forall\hat{k}\in[K]}\Bigg(f\Big(e_{k}^{1}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\prod_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\prod_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{\hat{k}}}\text{Pr}(e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}|\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}})\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}B_{k}\mathbb{E}_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\sim\emph{Multi($\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}$)}}\Bigg(f\Big(e_{k}^{1}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,1)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{b=1}^{n_{k}}\mathbb{E}_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\sim\emph{Multi($\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}$)}}\Bigg(f\Big(e_{k}^{b}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,b)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\Bigg),

where the fourth equality follows from
\text{Pr}(v_{k}^{m}|\mathbf{p}_{k})=p_{k}^{m}
; the seventh equality comes from
e_{k}^{1}\sim\text{Multi}(\mathbf{p}_{k})
and
f(\emptyset|B)=0
for any
B\subseteq\mathcal{V}
as well as the final equality follows from that each
e_{k}^{b}
is independently drawn from the multinoulli distribution
\text{Multi}(\mathbf{p}_{k})
.

If
f
is
\beta
-weakly submodular from above, we can show that

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{b=1}^{n_{k}}f\Big(e_{k}^{b}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,b)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\leq\beta\Bigg(f\Big(\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{\hat{k}}}\Big)-f(\emptyset)\Bigg)\leq\beta f\Big(\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{\hat{k}}}\Big),

where the final inequality follows from
f(\emptyset)\geq 0
(Note that we define
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
).

As a result, when
f
is
\beta
-weakly submodular from above, we have that

\displaystyle\Big\langle(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}),\nabla F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{b=1}^{n_{k}}\mathbb{E}_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\sim\emph{Multi($\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}$)}}\Bigg(f\Big(e_{k}^{b}|\cup_{(\hat{k},\hat{b})\neq(k,b)}\{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\}\Big)\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\beta\mathbb{E}_{e_{\hat{k}}^{\hat{b}}\sim\text{Multi($\mathbf{p}_{\hat{k}}$)}}\Bigg(f\Big(\cup_{\hat{k}=1}^{K}\cup_{\hat{b}=1}^{B_{\hat{k}}}\Big)\Bigg)=\beta F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}).

Note that an
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular function automatically satisfies the conditions for being
\frac{1}{\alpha}
-weakly submodular from above. Thus, we get the Eq.(
24
).
∎

Merging Theorem
10
into Theorem
2
, we also can get that

Theorem 11
.

When the set function
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is monotone and
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular, for any subset
S
within the partition constraint of problem (
1
) and any point
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\in\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
, the following inequality holds:

\left\langle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{B_{k}}\mathbf{1}_{S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k}}-\Big(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}\Big),\nabla F\Big(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}\Big)\right\rangle\geq\alpha f(S)-(\alpha+\frac{1}{\alpha})F\Big(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}\Big).

Similarly, when the set function
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is monotone and
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular, for any subset
S
within the partition constraint of problem (
1
) and any point
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\in\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
, we also can infer that

\left\langle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{B_{k}}\mathbf{1}_{S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k}}-\Big(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}\Big),\nabla F\Big(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}\Big)\right\rangle\geq\gamma^{2}f(S)-(\beta+\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2})F\Big(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}\Big).

From the definition of stationary point, we know that if
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K})\in\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
is the stationary point over the domain
\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
, for any point
\mathbf{y}\in\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
,

\left\langle\mathbf{y}-\Big(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}\Big),\nabla F\Big(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}\Big)\right\rangle\leq 0.

(25)

Also, for any
S
within the partition constraint of problem (
1
), we can easily show that
\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{B_{k}}\mathbf{1}_{S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k}}\in\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
such that we know that, for any
S
within the partition constraint of problem (
1
),

\left\langle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{B_{k}}\mathbf{1}_{S\cap\mathcal{V}_{k}}-\Big(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}\Big),\nabla F\Big(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}\Big)\right\rangle\leq 0.

Therefore, when the set function
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is monotone and
\alpha
-weakly DR-submodular, we have that

\alpha f(S)-(\alpha+\frac{1}{\alpha})F\Big(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}\Big)\leq 0
such that
F\Big(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}\Big)\geq\frac{\alpha^{2}}{1+\alpha^{2}}f(S^{*})
where
S^{*}
is the optimal solution of problem (
1
). Similarly, when the set function
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is monotone and
(\gamma,\beta)
-weakly submodular,, we have that
\gamma^{2}f(S)-(\beta+\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2})F\Big(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}\Big)\leq 0
such that
F\Big(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{K}\Big)\geq\Big(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\beta+\beta(1-\gamma)+\gamma^{2}}\Big)f(S^{*})
.

D-B

A Multinoulli Extension with
1/2
-Approximation Stationary Point

In this subsection, we consider a special set function. At first, let the universe set
U
consist of
n-1
elements
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1}\}
and
n-k
elements
\{y_{1},\dots,y_{n-k}\}
, all of weight
1
, and
n-1
elements
\{\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{n-1}\}
of arbitrarily
small weight
\epsilon>0
. Then, we define two different types of sets namely,
A_{i}
and
A_{i+n}
for any
i\in[n]
, that is to say ,

\displaystyle A_{i}\triangleq\{\epsilon_{i}\}\text{\ for $1\leq i\leq n-1$,}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ A_{n}\triangleq\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1}\},

\displaystyle A_{n+i}\triangleq\{x_{i}\}\text{\ for $1\leq i\leq n-1$,}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ A_{2n}\triangleq\{y_{1},\dots,y_{n-k}\}.

After that, we define a coverage set function
f:2^{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
over these
2n
distinct set
\{A_{1},\dots,A_{n},A_{n+1},\dots,A_{2n}\}
where
\mathcal{V}=[2n]
. Specifically, for any subset
S\subseteq\mathcal{V}
, we have that

f(S)\triangleq\sum_{v\in\bigcup_{i\in S}A_{i}}w(v),

(26)

where
w(v)
is the weight of element
v
.

Moreover, we consider a partition constraint that contains at most one of
\{A_{i},A_{n+i}\}
for any
i\in[n]
. If we set
\mathcal{V}_{i}\triangleq\{i,i+n\}
and
\mathcal{V}\triangleq\bigcup_{i\in[n]}\mathcal{V}_{i}\triangleq[2n]
, we naturally obtain the following coverage maximization problem:

\max_{S\subseteq\mathcal{V}}f(S)\ \ \text{s.t.}\ |S\cap\mathcal{V}_{i}|\leq 1\ \ \forall i\in[n].

(27)

Note that this problem (
27
) is a special case of the concern subset selection problem (
1
). From the result of
(
filmus2012power
)
, we know that the coverage function
f
in Eq.(
26
) is a submodular set function, namely,
\alpha=\beta=\gamma=1
.

A key feature of the coverage maximization problem (
27
) is that
(
filmus2012power
)
found that the standard greedy
(
nemhauser1978analysis
)
will be stuck at a local maximum subset
\{A_{1},\dots,A_{n}\}
where
S=[n]
and
f(S)=(1+\epsilon)n
. In contrast, when
\epsilon
is very small, the optimal subset for the problem (
27
) is
\{A_{n+1},\dots,A_{2n}\}
where
S=\{n+1,\dots,2n\}
and
f(S)=2n-k-1
. Note that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\frac{(1+\epsilon)n}{2n-k-1}=\frac{1}{2}
.
Motivated by this finding of
(
filmus2012power
)
, we also can show that the
point
\mathbf{1}_{[n]}\triangleq(\underbrace{1,\dots,1}_{n},0,\dots,0)
is a local stationary point of multinoulli extension(
ME
) of the set function
f
in Eq.(
26
). More specifically, we have the following theorem:

Theorem 12
.

The point
\mathbf{1}_{[n]}
is a stationary point of the multinoulli extension
F
of the set function
f
for problem (
27
). Furthermore, we can show
\frac{F(\mathbf{1}_{[n]})}{f(\{n+1,\dots,2n\})}=\frac{(1+\epsilon)n}{2n-k-1}\rightarrow\frac{1}{2}
.

Remark 18
.

Theorem

12
indicates that when the objective set function is submodular, namely,
c=\alpha=\gamma=\beta=1
, the approximation guarantees established in
Theorem

3
is
tight
.

Proof.

From the problem (
27
), we know that each
\mathbf{p}_{i}=(p_{i}^{1},p_{i}^{2})
. Then, from
Definition

1
, we can show that

F(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{e_{i}^{1}\in\{i,i+n,\emptyset\}}\Bigg(f\big(\cup_{i=1}^{n}\{e_{i}^{1}\}\big)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\text{Pr}(e_{i}^{1}|\mathbf{p}_{i})\Bigg),

(28)

where
\text{Pr}(i|\mathbf{p}_{i})=p_{i}^{1}
,
\text{Pr}(n+i|\mathbf{p}_{i})=p_{i}^{2}
and
\text{Pr}(\emptyset|\mathbf{p}_{i})=1-p_{i}^{1}-p_{i}^{2}
.

Moreover, from the part
1)
of
Theorem

1
, we know that,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial F}{\partial(p_{i}^{1})}(\mathbf{1}_{[n]})=\epsilon\text{\ for $1\leq i\leq n-1$,}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \frac{\partial F}{\partial(p_{n}^{1})}(\mathbf{1}_{[n]})=n-1,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial F}{\partial(p_{i}^{2})}(\mathbf{1}_{[n]})=0\text{\ for $1\leq i\leq n-1$,}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \frac{\partial F}{\partial(p_{n}^{2})}(\mathbf{1}_{[n]})=n-k.

As a result, for any
(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{n})\in\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Delta_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{n})-\mathbf{1}_{[n]},\nabla F(\mathbf{1}_{[n]})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Bigg(p_{i}^{1}\frac{\partial F}{\partial(p_{i}^{1})}(\mathbf{1}_{[n]})+p_{i}^{2}\frac{\partial F}{\partial(p_{i}^{2})}(\mathbf{1}_{[n]})\Bigg)-(1+\epsilon)(n-1)

\displaystyle=\epsilon\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}p_{i}^{1}+(n-1)p_{n}^{1}+(n-k)p_{n}^{2}-(1+\epsilon)(n-1)

\displaystyle=\epsilon\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(p_{i}^{1}-1)+(n-1)p_{n}^{1}+(n-k)p_{n}^{2}-(n-1)

\displaystyle=\epsilon\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(p_{i}^{1}-1)+\big((n-k)-(n-1)\big)p_{n}^{2}-(n-1)(1-p_{n}^{1}-p_{n}^{2})\leq 0.

As a result,
\mathbf{1}_{[n]}
is a stationary point of the
ME

F
. Note that
\frac{F(\mathbf{1}_{[n]})}{F(\mathbf{1}_{\{n+1,\dots,2n\}})}=\frac{(1+\epsilon)(n-1)}{2n-1-k}\rightarrow\frac{1}{2}
when
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
and
n\rightarrow\infty
, which implies that the approximation guarantee of
Theorem

3
is tight when
\alpha=\beta=\gamma=1
.
∎

D-C

Proof of
Theorem

4

In this subsection, we prove the
Theorem

4
. Before that, we firstly prove the following lemma:

Lemma 1
.

Given a monotone set function
f
, for its multinoulli extension
F
introduced in
Definition

1
, if we consider an auxiliary function
F^{a}:\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
whose gradient at each point
\mathbf{x}\in\prod_{k=1}^{K}\Delta_{n_{k}}
